cs.AI, cs.HC

Research on sycophancy asks whether large language models (LLMs) bend towards what their users want to hear. We ask the reverse question: when an LLM is confident, consistent, and wrong, do users bend towards it? In a within-subjects experiment, 32 participants learned up to five science topics by chatting with five assistants built on the same model (GPT-5-mini). Unknown to participants, each assistant's briefing had 0, 25, 50, 75, or 100% of its facts replaced by plausible falsehoods, and every assistant spoke with full confidence. A surprise quiz after each topic offered the correct fact, the planted falsehood, and two distractors, with a confidence rating for every answer. Across 104 quizzes and 1,040 answers, scores fell from 7.4 to 2.9 out of 10, and the planted option was chosen on 60% of falsified items against 11% of correctly taught ones (odds ratio 12.5). Transcript coding showed that whenever the assistant stated a falsehood, it was adopted about 65% of the time at every falsification level. Absorbed falsehoods were held with high confidence (8.4/10), the modeled confidence advantage of right over wrong answers vanished, and the discrimination of confidence for correctness fell from an AUROC of 0.70 to chance, while post-task trust ratings barely moved. Participants tended to return to the chat more when the assistant was worse, but returning went with more absorption, not less, and 30% of all turns pasted quiz questions back to the source being checked. The assistant itself was almost never sycophantic and never conceded under pushback. We introduce a baseline-corrected Human Deference Index to quantify this human-side sycophancy and argue that trustworthiness is a property of the human-AI pair, that sycophancy evaluations should measure both directions, and that learning interfaces must make reliability legible claim by claim.

Simulated Control Evals

Jeffrey Olmo, David Zhang, Tyler Tracy

cs.AI

Control evaluations measure whether safeguards can prevent an AI model from causing undesired outcomes even when it is intentionally trying to do so. These evaluations typically use purpose-built control settings and extrapolate their results to a model's deployment. However, the effectiveness of safeguards in deployment can depend on features of environments and tasks that control settings do not represent. To broaden where control evaluations can be run, we introduce simulated control evaluations (SCEs), which use a language model to simulate tool responses in place of an environment. We evaluate SCEs on LinuxArena, where execution provides ground-truth outcomes. We compare SCE prediction errors with those from a separate control setting, BashArena, and with variation in control metrics within LinuxArena. For independently sampled evaluation and deployment task sets, SCEs predict safety with a root mean squared error of 3.6 percentage points across five monitors at a 5% audit budget, compared with 10.1 for BashArena. SCEs closely reproduce distributions of attack strategies, attacker reasoning, and monitor reasoning, with over tenfold lower KL divergence from execution than BashArena. SCEs explain 78-85% of the between-environment variance in attack success, monitor scores, and safety. This accuracy is sample efficient: SCEs predict safety with less error than BashArena using fewer than 5% as many task-pair draws as the deployment task set. Strategies discovered through simulation also improve monitor performance on held-out LinuxArena attacks, outperforming BashArena and approximately matching improvement from executed strategies. These results suggest that deployment-specific SCEs can be more predictive than control evaluations executed in separate control settings.

EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence

Qijin Zhang, Hanzhen Wang, Yuehan Zhao, Haoyu Dai, Yao Shu, Zhongxiang Dai, Shuang Qiu

cs.AI, cs.LG

Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.

Direct Pass@$k$ Optimization for Thought Pruning: Balancing Quality and Diversity in Test-Time Reasoning

Mahdi Salmani, Adel Javanmard, Devansh Gupta, Meisam Razaviyayn

cs.AI, cs.CL

Test-time scaling has emerged as a key inference strategy for reasoning models, where a model generates a large number of candidate solutions in the hope of finding at least one correct solution. Running all candidate solutions to completion can be computationally demanding, so recent work prunes the candidate pool after generating only a short prefix, committing further computation only to the surviving candidates. Existing pruning strategies divide broadly into two categories: Quality-based methods assign a score to each partial solution and discard those predicted to ultimately lead to an incorrect final solution, while diversity-based methods select partial solutions that follow different reasoning trajectories and are therefore likely to cover different final solutions. The tension between these two objectives is an instance of exploration versus exploitation, yet little to no prior work on pruning exposes this trade-off directly, let alone optimizes it. In this paper, we introduce a pruning procedure that addresses this trade-off directly by optimizing pass@$k$ over the selected solution pool. In particular, it chooses the pool to maximize the probability that at least one of its partial solutions, when continued to completion, yields a correct answer. Because this objective simultaneously rewards high-quality partial solutions and penalizes redundancy among them, quality and diversity are naturally balanced by the criterion itself rather than through separate ad-hoc design choices. On eight open-weight reasoning models, our method outperforms existing quality-based and diversity-based pruning strategies. The gains are particularly pronounced when the pruning procedure keeps a moderate number of partial solutions for continued generation, highlighting the value of directly balancing quality and diversity during pruning.

baseVultra: Compact Nonlinear Recurrence with Reallocated Capacity

Linke Zhang

cs.CL, cs.LG, from, WAIC, Academic, 2026, (1, weak, accept,, 2, borderline)

Nonlinear recurrent networks remain useful for streaming sequence modeling, yet gated cell design is still dominated by LSTM and GRU. We study whether recurrent computation can be reallocated away from gate transforms and toward candidate generation. We propose baseVultra, a compact recurrent cell that concentrates the dense recurrent budget on a single H x H candidate-generation matrix, while replacing recurrent gate matrices with O(H) diagonal interactions. The candidate update combines this dense branch with two low-rank auxiliary branches, an EMA cache, and learnable output blending; the gates additionally receive lightweight second-order access to h_{t-2} through diagonal terms. On TinyShakespeare character-level language modeling, baseVultra achieves a validation loss of 1.594 in the compact one-layer 200K setting, compared with 1.604 for a parameter-matched LSTM and 1.616 for a parameter-matched GRU, while its dense recurrent matrix-vector computation is about 54% of GRU under matched hidden size for this configuration. On text8, a roughly 45x larger benchmark, baseVultra is competitive with full-gate cells (1.854 bpc vs. 1.849 for GRU and 1.837 for LSTM). On formal-language probes, the largest gains appear under constrained hidden-state capacity (0.991 vs. 0.761 GRU and 0.691 LSTM OOD2 accuracy on Counter at H=16) and vanish as hidden size grows. These results characterize where and when candidate-focused recurrent capacity allocation helps: the benefit is concentrated in compact regimes, while full-gate cells remain competitive at scale.

Lighthouse: On Building a Trustworthy Digital Assistant for Exploring History with Civil Rights Archives

John A. Aydin, Angelica Williams, Steven Dindl, Nathan Saunders, Kate Boyd, Vandana Srivastava, Biplav Srivastava

cs.CL, cs.AI

Artificial intelligence (AI), as a decision-support aid, provides new tools for studying history effectively using multimodal data, including text, images, and audio. We present, to our knowledge, the first digital assistant named Lighthouse--commonly called a chatbot--for exploring the Civil Rights period of the United States using data from South Carolina. From an AI perspective, the system's novelty lies in its guiding design principles of (a) focusing on trustworthiness of content and (b) by modeling and reducing potential harm to users from generated answers. It implements these with a neuro-symbolic approach that combines neural learning through a large language model (LLM) with rule-based symbolic solving, balancing risk of answers with generality of coverage. We evaluate Lighthouse with both factual objective questions that admit a single acceptable answer as well as subjective questions. For the latter, a dataset of subjective question-answer pairs (QAs) is created by discovering a "gold standard" by aggregating answers from independent experts. Lighthouse has access to over 18k primary sources from a range of collections and can be easily extended.

TauIndianBankBench: Procedure-Aware, Action-Oriented Evaluation of Tool-Using Conversational Agents

Krishanu Adhikary, Prashant Devadiga

cs.CL, cs.AI, https://github.com/npci/TauIndianBankBench;, data:, https://huggingface.co/datasets/NPCI/tau-indian-banking

Many Indian banks serve customers through FAQ pages and scripted chatbots. Assistants based on language models could respond faster and carry out requests such as blocking a card, but only if they give correct information and follow the bank's rules. These rules prescribe a procedure, a set of required steps, such as obtaining a closure quote before closing a deposit. Adapting models to this domain requires measuring how well they follow the procedure, before and after training. Benchmarks usually judge only the records left at the end of a conversation. Many required steps, however, leave the records unchanged, so an assistant that skips them can still be judged successful. We present TauIndianBankBench, an action-oriented benchmark of 1,000 Indian retail-banking tasks where an assistant helps a simulated customer. Besides the final records, it checks that the assistant took every required step and made no unrequested change. For every model, 6.5-12.0% of sessions are judged successful when only the final records are checked, although the assistant skipped a required step or made an unrequested change. Scoring both procedure and outcome shows more accurately how well language models perform in Indian retail banking.

FusionEval: Benchmarking Model Fusion for Large Language Models

Shuo Cai, Yanggan Gu, Yuhang Liu, Su Lu, Yuanyi Wang, Yang Yu, Zhen Li, Zhitian Hou, Guanghao Zhu, Ming Li, Hongxia Yang

cs.CL, cs.AI

Model fusion aims to integrate the capabilities of multi-expert models into a single model. Existing evaluations offer limited coverage of reasoning with long chains of thought and tool use, while strong average performance can mask substantial losses in individual expert capabilities. We introduce FusionEval, a benchmark that provides controlled expert pools, integrates fusion methods with and without additional training, and offers a unified evaluation suite. The pools contain domain experts independently trained from Qwen3 and Llama base models across math, code, science, instruction following, and tool use. The suite evaluates capability retention and computational cost on eight public benchmarks under comparable data access rules, reporting normalized average and worst performance, runtime, and peak GPU memory usage. Extensive experiments show that training-free methods preserve reasoning performance but substantially degrade tool use at both model scales. In contrast, MOPD preserves tool use and achieves a more balanced capability profile, but requires additional data preparation, inference, and training. Jointly retaining reasoning, instruction following, and tool use at low computational cost remains a challenge. We hope FusionEval supports future research on efficient model fusion that jointly preserves expert capabilities. Our code is available at https://github.com/Baicaihaochi/FusionEval_Benchmark.

Measuring Research Progress and Linguistic Inclusion in Low-Resource Languages

Atnafu Lambebo Tonja, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Jesujoba Oluwadara Alabi, Israel Abebe Azime, Benjamin Rosman, Ehsan Shareghi, Thamar Solorio, Pontus Stenetorp, Monojit Choudhury

cs.CL

Large Language Models (LLMs) have accelerated research on low-resource and multilingual NLP, but their effectiveness in creating an equitable landscape remains unmapped. We analyze 1,158 peer-reviewed open-access papers published between 2020 and 2025, examining research attention to low-resource languages (LRLs) and representation across language and speaker populations. To assess linguistic inclusion beyond publication counts, we introduce the Linguistic Inclusion Framework (LIF), a multidimensional framework that measures linguistic inclusion in four dimensions: research growth, representation expansion, representation continuity, and distributional equity. While annual publications grew from 52 to 415, a 5.4X increase in their ACL Anthology publication share, this growth largely concentrated on represented languages rather than expanding coverage of underrepresented languages. Further analysis shows that languages spoken in Asia account for 64.1% of speakers across LRLs we analyze but receive only 46.7% of the language representation share (0.7X their proportional share), while European languages receive 5.3X their proportional share. At the country level, linguistically diverse regions such as sub-Saharan Africa and Southeast Asia show the lowest language coverage ratios. Applying the framework at the continental level, we find that no region scores well on all four dimensions, showing that research growth alone is not a reliable metric of progress toward equitable and inclusive language technology\footnote{We will release all collected and annotated data for research purposes}.

AQL-Lite: Enabling Efficient Knowledge Acquisition for Arabic LLMs via Data-Centric Continual Pretraining

Houssam Eddine Boukhalfa, Mourad Bouache, Mourad Dahmane

cs.CL, cs.LG

Training Arabic large language models faces challenges from noisy web data, dialectal variation, and the high cost of continual pretraining (CPT). We introduce AQL-Lite (AQL: Arabic Quality Language), a time- and resource-efficient framework for adapting a multilingual LLM to Modern Standard Arabic (MSA). AQL-Lite couples a quality-first curation pipeline (structural cleaning, heuristic and model-based filtering, MSA selection, and deduplication) with a partial-parameter CPT recipe that trains RS-LoRA adapters across the network together with the last eight transformer blocks and the embedding matrix, under layer-wise learning rates. At a matched 1B-token budget, CPT on AQL-curated data raises a four-benchmark Arabic mean by 3.51 points over CPT on raw web text, which yields no gain over the base model. In a controlled 5B-token comparison, AQL-Lite recovers 94% of the gain of full-parameter CPT (mean 73.19 vs. 73.58) in 52% of the wall-clock time and outperforms adapter-only baselines on every benchmark, and a component ablation shows that adapters, unfrozen blocks, and embedding training each contribute. Scaled to 40B MSA tokens and instruction-tuned on one million examples, the resulting 12B model scores 75.3 on the seven-benchmark Open Arabic LLM Leaderboard v2 (OALL v2) suite. These results suggest that partial-parameter CPT on a carefully curated corpus is a practical route to efficient MSA adaptation.

Consistency Is Not Validity: Self-Rewarded Moral Argumentation Makes Language Models Decisive but Pliable

Marius De Kuthy Meurers, Aaron Parisi, Bernd Bohnet

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG

LLMs are increasingly consulted for moral judgment. We aim to improve performance on such tasks by rewarding good arguments, on the premise that arguing well increases the likelihood of judging well. We train a reinforcement learning policy initialized from a state-of-the-art language model in a self-judged tournament with masked thinking: the generated arguments are judged pairwise on argument quality by the policy itself. As an empirical basis, we extract authentic dilemmas from classic novels, using a logic solver to verify that each constitutes a conflict. We show that our judge rates argument quality like human annotators, and the policy then adopts the language register that it rewards. Testing on the external benchmarks MoralChoice, ELEPHANT, and MORABLES, we find that the policy becomes more decisive and invariant to the order in which options are presented, but sycophancy and reframing remain a challenge to validity. Two ablation studies with synthetic dilemmas and unmasked thinking match the reordering gains, but show a weaker register shift and higher sycophancy. We conclude that consistency is not validity: consistency under reordering can be optimized without improving the moral judgment it is meant to signal.

CiteAnchor: Source-Aware Grounding for Hallucinated Citation Detection and Resolution

Chenyi Tong, Ping Nie, Yu Zhang

cs.CL, cs.DL

Hallucinated citations make it harder to trace sources and verify the evidence behind scientific claims. Detecting them is difficult because bibliographic records can be incomplete or differ across source types and publication versions. A plausible match alone neither validates the citation nor establishes its underlying source. We introduce CiteResolve, a benchmark of 423 real-world hallucinated citations annotated for source recoverability. We further propose CiteAnchor, a source-aware grounding framework that adapts retrieval and field-level verification to each source type and separately determines citation validity and whether a unique source can be recovered. On an existing detection benchmark, CiteAnchor achieves 0.841 F1 with GPT-5.6 Luna and 0.784 with an open-weight backbone, outperforming the baseline with the same backbones by 7.9 and 36.8 points. On CiteResolve, it achieves 74.7% and 70.7% end-to-end task success, outperforming the corresponding web search baselines by 16.3 and 65.3 percentage points. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/oldwizard7/CiteANCHOR_code.

Climbing the Ladder: Automating Data Pipelines and Reproducing Baselines in Low-Resource Settings

Donato Meoli, Eryk Wdowiak

cs.CL

The massively multilingual translation systems that now include Sicilian learned it from noisy, non-standardised web text. To obtain better translation quality, we redevelop the small neural machine translation (NMT) system that Wdowiak customized for Sicilian. We rebuild on maintained software the extraction of the parallel text from the PDFs of the bilingual journal Arba Sicula, whose sentences are aligned by a language-agnostic sentence encoder with thresholds tuned against an existing hand alignment and then filtered for the typical errors of automatic alignment. Furthermore, the tokenization and the desinence-biased subword segmentation of the original system are ported to a Perl-free implementation, and all the results are measured on a frozen test set drawn from the journal. We compare small Transformers trained from scratch with the pretrained NLLB model adapted by low-rank fine-tuning, in both directions and with Italian as a closely related bridge language, and we adapt to the pretrained model the reverse-training recipe of the original system, i.e., a stage on forward-scored back-translations of the web Sicilian followed by a stage on the curated literary text. The pretrained model is by far the better of the two, and the staged training improves the translation into Sicilian more than the translation from it. On the test set of the original system, and in the tokenized space in which it was scored, the staged model with a larger adapter and more back-translations is above the reverse-training recipe in the directions that involve English and below it between Sicilian and Italian. Yet, a multilingual adapter degrades the directions on which it was not trained, which limits its use as a general multilingual model.

MemPlan: Memory-Conditioned PDDL Planning for Partially Observable Text Environments

Tengwei Song, Robert Hoehndorf

cs.CL, cs.AI

LLM agents are flexible in language interaction, but using them as long-horizon planners is costly and prone to hallucinated or invalid actions. This paper targets a different role for LLMs: constrained bridges between language and symbolic planning. We propose MemPlan, a memory-conditioned PDDL planning framework for partially observable interactive text environments. MemPlan keeps the PDDL domain fixed and reconstructs the PDDL problem at each step from parsed observations, candidate hypotheses, and interaction memory. Positive graph memory assigns relevance-based action costs to candidate hypotheses for missing objects, locations, or conditions, guiding the planner toward more plausible checks. Negative memory records execution-refuted assumptions and compiles them into exclusion constraints, preventing repeated attempts at candidates already contradicted by feedback. A unified schema-conditioned language--symbol interface, trained offline with supervised fine-tuning, connects text environments to the planning loop through observation-to-fact parsing and symbolic-action-to-command grounding. Experiments on TextWorld, ALFWorld, and Robotouille show that MemPlan improves task completion and step efficiency over language-only and PDDL-aware baselines, while substantially reducing online token cost compared with LLM-planning baselines such as ReAct. The same fine-tuned interface is also reused across TextWorld task types without task-specific fine-tuning. Our code and data are available at: https://github.com/tewiSong/MemPlan.

SIM-UT: A User Simulation based Evaluation Framework for Medical Triage Chatbots

Deepali Mittal, Dheeraj Mundhra, Yulia Sidorova, Tersia de Kock, Oli Munns, Sofie S Meyer

cs.CL

LLM-based chat agents are increasingly used for complex, multi-turn, multilingual medical triage in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs), yet evaluation methods focus on single-turn, English-only benchmarks that primarily test the chatbots' knowledge. In practice, users are often confused, overwhelmed, or emotionally vulnerable, giving unclear, misspelled single-word answers or long messages in regional dialects, and the context of a conversation can shift rapidly. We propose SIM-UT (Simulation of User Behaviour for Triage), a multi-turn, simulation-based evaluation method that identifies ecologically valid, contextually relevant failure modes of LLM-based triage chat agents. Using SIM-UT, we evaluated three real-world WhatsApp-based health chatbots: a respiratory screening programme in India, a hearing health support programme in South Africa, and a diabetes and lifestyle coaching agent in Kenya. We compared a medically fine-tuned open model (MedGemma) against general-purpose frontier models (ChatGPT and Claude) across 5,000+ multilingual simulated conversation turns grounded in the personas, constraints, and communication patterns of the real user populations, over 2-4 iterative rounds. A key failure mode emerged in both model families: de-escalation risk. The agents initially identified medical emergencies correctly, but when the user minimised the symptoms without introducing new clinical information, they de-escalated, exhibiting clinically dangerous sycophancy, for example agreeing that vaginal bleeding in pregnancy was "not a problem" or that decreased fetal movements at 32 weeks "can be normal". We recommend user simulation-based behavioural evaluation, grounded in real user context and covering adversarial and edge cases, as standard practice for triage chatbots in production, so that safeguards can be added before deployment or in subsequent iterations.

Self-Assessment Reinforcement Learning for Search-State Awareness in Retrieval-Augmented Large Language Models

Taewhoo Lee, Minju Song, Jungwoo Park, Chanwoong Yoon, Junha Jung, Hyeon Hwang, Sanghoon Lee, Minbyul Jeong, Jaewoo Kang

cs.CL, cs.AI

Recent advances in search agents have enabled large language models to tackle complex tasks by retrieving and synthesizing external information. However, existing search agents often lack search-state awareness, i.e., the ability to recognize what information has been obtained and what remains missing, leading to hallucinated reasoning and prematurely terminated search. To address this limitation, we propose Self-Assessment Reinforcement Learning (SARL), a reinforcement learning framework in which models learn to recognize their own search state during training and improve their search behavior accordingly, without requiring additional assessment at test time. SARL achieves this through a dual reward design that captures the corrective effect of each assessment while penalizing excessive reliance on it, jointly encouraging effective and efficient search trajectories. Under this setup, SARL achieves a 24.3% relative improvement over the strongest baseline, with average gains of 35.9% on multi-hop reasoning and 27.9% on deep research benchmarks against the strongest per-benchmark baselines. SARL further surpasses methods built on auxiliary supervision or multi-agent coordination, despite operating as a standalone RL framework with no inference-time scaffolding. Beyond final performance, SARL maintains training stability under extended search horizons, a regime where existing methods consistently degrade, enabling sustained gains as task complexity grows.

Rethinking the Evaluation of LLM Fact-Checkers

Ayana Niwa, Miyu Yamada, Kouta Nakayama, Masahiro Kaneko, Hisami Suzuki, Satoshi Sekine

cs.CL

Large language models (LLMs) are widely used to answer factual questions, but their answers can contain errors and must be fact-checked claim by claim. Yet the resulting score depends on three design decisions: which questions the claims answer, under what conditions each claim is judged, and which sources count as evidence. No existing benchmark specifies all three. We introduce JSFactCheckBench, a Japanese benchmark that specifies all three, with 1,913 claims from LLM answers to questions about misleading information that circulated on Japanese social media, each annotated for factuality at a reference time and for importance to the question, using evidence from preselected Web sources. Across six LLMs, all verify claims directly answering the question less accurately than supplementary claims, a gap that aggregate scores obscure. Scores also drop when models must locate the relevant content in longer or multiple sources and, for all four open models, when the surrounding question and response are added. These weaknesses are visible only because the benchmark records the conditions behind each label. A benchmark that leaves them implicit cannot reveal them.

Behind Aggregate Scores: What Systematically Varying Arena-Hard's LLM Judge Reveals

Louise Yorke

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY, 2026, (non-archival)., 18, pages,, 3, figures., Code, and, data:, https://github.com/Yorkel/llm-judge-release

LLM judges are used to determine which of two answers is better. Trusting these verdicts requires knowing how repeatable they are and how much they depend on the judge's instructions. We show that similar aggregate scores can conceal substantial changes in individual verdicts, both when the same setup is repeated and when the instructions change. Using 500 Arena-Hard v2.0 prompts with two models' answers held fixed, we run GPT-4.1 as the judge under all eight combinations of criteria, output format and task framing, and repeat every setup unchanged at temperature zero on a pinned model version. Rerunning the default setup moves the aggregate score by about one point but changes the win/tie/loss outcome on 21.2% of prompts. Instruction changes introduce further sensitivity: in one setup, which asks for the verdict without reasoning and also drops several other instructions, outcomes change on 28.2 percentage points more prompts than the two setups' unchanged reruns do on average, and the aggregate score falls by 4.09 points. In another, where the criteria prioritise accuracy, outcomes change on 6.7 percentage points more prompts by the same comparison, while the aggregate score barely moves. A stable aggregate score therefore does not establish that individual verdicts are repeatable. This distinction matters when those verdicts are used as preference labels or to assess individual responses. Evaluations that rely on individual verdicts should repeat each setup they use and report both aggregate scores and prompt-level agreement.

Protegrity-PII Technical Report

Hardeep Bassi, Hui Ma, Lu\'is Seabra, Koowum Joshi, Daniel Pereira, Averell Gatton

cs.CL, cs.AI

We present Protegrity-PII, a new PII decision model for structured and unstructured text. Protegrity-PII achieves a typed recall of 0.915 with a precision of 0.907 on a new benchmark we propose, PII-SCOPE. Our model processes text in 0.49 seconds per million characters at \$0.0005 per million characters on a single RTX 6000 using batched inference. Due in part to the expansive nature of the entity type taxonomy used in training, Protegrity-PII achieves superior out-of-distribution generalization when benchmarked against a selection of state of the art models and datasets.

IFC-MCQ: Automatically Generating Multiple-Choice Benchmarks for Evaluating LLM Agents on IFC Data Retrieval

Rabindra Lamsal, Sisi Zlatanova, Xuesong Shen

cs.CL

Industry Foundation Classes (IFC), the standard for building information exchange in architecture, engineering, and construction (AEC), is increasingly queried through large language models (LLMs) using natural language. Evaluating these systems at scale is difficult because free-text answers require costly human or LLM-based review. We propose IFC-MCQ, a method that automatically generates multiple-choice benchmarks from IFC files for exact option-set grading. The taxonomy covers 15 categories and 36 subtypes across four groups, with answer keys derived from the file and distractors constructed from file-derived values or predefined rules. We applied IFC-MCQ to IFC models of varying sizes and modelling characteristics to assess generation coverage and evaluated two agentic configurations on selected models using tool- and code-based retrieval. The evaluation exposed category-specific failure modes and showed that performance can vary with the source IFC model and retrieval interface. IFC-MCQ enables scalable benchmarking of LLM-based IFC retrieval in AEC workflows.

What the Specification Did Not Say: Specification-Oracle Gaps in Test-Graded Coding-Agent Benchmarks

Fei Ding

cs.CL

Coding-agent benchmarks often describe a task in natural language but grade it with tests taken from the upstream project. Passing those tests can require information the description never states: a constant's name, a module path, an exact message. We call this a specification-oracle gap: where it exists, a benchmark also measures whether an agent reproduces upstream implementation details. We formalise the gap, including the inconsistencies that chains of dependent tasks add, and build a static audit for five languages that classifies every name-, path- and literal-level dependency of every scored test (held-out precision 89.2%, recall 92.9%). On SWE-Milestone (98 graded milestones, 35 published runs), 25 milestones contain a test that depends on an unstated, non-inferable name or path. Build units with such a dependency fail in 80.6% of runs, against 48.4% for comparable units without one, and withholding a single hidden name from the reference stops its unit's tests from compiling in 126 of 128 cases. The leaderboard's ranking is robust to removing the affected tests, but they shift scores within repositories and favour agents that reproduce upstream names. Across four further benchmarks, prevalence appears to follow construction: a third or more of the tasks are affected where specifications are release notes or LLM summaries, almost none where tasks are screened or written for their tests; how much a gap costs depends on how the harness grades. We release the audit, corrected filter lists and specification addenda.

Compiling Autoregressive LLM Control into Policy-Exact Parallel Inference for Hierarchical RAG

Zehao Lin, Qianfeng Shen, Xixuan Hao, Shaobo Cui, Chunyu Li, Kai Chen, Bo Tang, Zhenjun Wang, Jin Yan, Zhiyu Li, Feiyu Xiong

cs.CL, cs.IR

Large language models increasingly act as controllers in structured decision pipelines, yet their decisions are still executed by autoregressive text decoding. Hierarchical RAG is one such setting: at each frontier of a document tree, an LLM commits to $b$ of $k$ sections under an input-dependent action grammar, executed as either failure-prone free-form JSON or reliable but strictly serial grammar-constrained generation. We show that this serialization is unnecessary for bounded-horizon control: the reachable future forms a shallow action-prefix tree, which we compile --- one root prefill plus one tree-causal probe evaluate every prefix-conditioned action distribution with serial decoding's tokens, positions, and causal ancestry. A prefix-state lemma makes compiled control policy-exact in exact arithmetic: the same policy with a two-forward critical path and zero output tokens. One further probe pass certifies the exact order-marginalized MAP set. Across four document-disjoint splits and two scales on the primary MetaX device, compiled control matches the serial policy on $84$--$89\%$ of questions --- every instrumented divergence a floating-point near-tie --- and cuts control latency by $35$--$47\%$ versus token-wise serial decoding and $6$--$7\%$ versus an idealized chunked engine; an H800 repetition preserves the ordering.

Spectral Smoothing and Spiking Perturbations Reconfigure Capability Profiles and Selective Task Performance in Large Language Models

Qince Luan, Jianhui Xu, and Yang Xu

cs.CL, cs.AI

Singular-value spectra encode important structure in transformer feed-forward projections, yet their causal role in downstream behavior remains underexplored. We study post-hoc spectral smoothing and spiking interventions on the up_proj and down_proj matrices. Positive perturbation stretches deviations from the geometric mean, making large singular values larger and small ones smaller (spiking), and vice versa. The intervention preserves singular vectors and the geometric mean of the singular values. Across five open Llama and Qwen instruction models, we evaluate the perturbed systems on capability-labeled subtasks, standard and chain-of-thought reasoning benchmarks, judge-scored truthfulness, hallucination, and safety tasks, and TRAIT personality probes. To interpret score shifts, we fit responses in a three-dimensional capability basis consisting of factual knowledge, language understanding, and deductive reasoning. The induced changes suggest structured rather than purely random sensitivity: large positive perturbations more reliably suppress language and deductive responses, moderate negative perturbations can sometimes improve factual or task-local performance on the evaluated grids, and sensitivity varies strongly by model family, benchmark module, and underlying layerwise spectral inequality. These results position spectral perturbation as a lightweight intervention for probing how capabilities are organized and traded off in large language models.

WikiOriginQA: Automated Construction of a QA Dataset with Knowledge Origins for Analyzing Cultural Bias

Kento Haneda, Yosuke Kishinami, Ryo Fujii, Keisuke Sakaguchi, Makoto Morishita

cs.CL

Existing datasets on cultural knowledge have been typically created by human annotators familiar with each culture. However, the process requires considerable human effort, presenting a significant challenge to scalability. To address this challenge, we introduce WikiOriginQA, a QA dataset automatically constructed by objectively representing the origins of knowledge through the creation dates of different Wikipedia language editions. Experimental results with the created dataset confirm that LLM performance tends to be biased toward Anglophone cultures. Furthermore, the results suggest the importance of knowledge propagation into the Anglosphere. These findings demonstrate the validity of our automated approach, enabling cultural bias evaluations with superior scalability across diverse languages without extensive manual annotation.

Direct cueing of word identity through word duration: evidence from Mandarin homophones

Xiaomeng Zhu, Megan Niu, Jason Shaw, Kevin Tang

cs.CL

While there is increasing evidence that words are stored in the mental lexicon with some phonetic detail, there are open questions about exactly which phonetic details are stored and whether they are functionally relevant for spoken word recognition. Word-level phonetic cues, such as word duration, provide an appropriate test case. Across languages, including Mandarin Chinese, high frequency words are shorter than low frequency words. Using a homophone ordering task, we investigate whether differences in word duration impact word recognition. Mandarin Chinese was examined since it has abundant disyllabic homophones. Our results indicate that participants were able to use word duration to recognize the order of homophone pairs, selected to vary in lexical frequency. Both with high noise levels and when no other cues were available, participants mapped the shorter duration auditory stimulus to the higher frequency word. The results provide evidence that word duration can directly cue the identity of words, suggesting that it may be a phonetic detail incorporated into the lexical representation. We discuss this direct cueing account alongside alternative accounts of how word duration may modulate word recognition.

From Clinical Judgment to Checkable Rules: Clinician-Led Guardrails for Mental-Health Chatbots

Chenhan Lyu, Kit Wislocki, Sarah Elizabeth Stoeckl, Maitreyi Sinha, Amir Rahmani

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY

Large language models (LLMs) deployed in mental-health contexts can be evaluated for safety failures, but the resulting judgments are often difficult to inspect or explain. Common approaches to improving safety rely on prompt engineering, LLM-as-a-Judge, and Constitutional AI. In this work, we describe a clinician-led alternative in which two clinicians define, finalize, and adjudicate the four safety checks of a rule base, which is formally verified for internal consistency and applied as a guardrail over LLM outputs. Here and in the title, clinician-led refers to this rule base, not to the fact extractor or the uncertainty default, which are ours. Every verdict of the resulting verification layer is recomputable from a named rule and sixteen facts. Against an LLM judge matched in model, revision, quantization, rule text, and decoding but without the layer's computed 988/911 fact, the largest disagreement class, the satisfied-rule class, is structured: the Llama-3.3-70B judge, on its primary prompt, demands a crisis resource from 55 of the 65 replies (84.6%) that already contain one, while the same model, asked as a fact extractor, reports a recognized resource present on 54 of the 55. The verification layer never raises that demand, by construction. We report with equal prominence where this judge is stronger: fewer false crisis alarms on the one reference-scored check. We also report the measured cost (a specification revision raised block-class outcomes on held-out boundary prompts of an over-refusal benchmark from 6.0% to 16.0%, chiefly by changing fact extraction), the clinicians' decision to keep our uncertainty default, and the analysis of the result. The contribution is not a safer verdict. It is a guardrail whose disagreements with the judge localize to named rules and facts and whose measured costs trace to named facts and the uncertainty default.

ULME: Predictive Coupling for Mutually Reinforcing Embedding and Language Modeling

Yang Liu, Zhiqiang Yuan, Shu-Dong Huang, Jiancheng Lv, Liwei Yang, Qiwei Yan, Kailin Lv, Jinchao Zhang

cs.CL, cs.AI

Text embedding and text generation are two of the most useful capabilities of modern large language models, yet they are typically provided by separate specialized models (an embedder and a generator) trained with incompatible objectives and deployed side by side in retrieval-augmented generation and agent systems. Recent unified embedding-generation models such as GritLM share parameters between the two capabilities, but they either switch attention regimes between modes or optimize embedding and language modeling as parallel objectives that never supervise each other. We argue that these designs are only partially unified: the two capabilities coexist on the same weights, but do not shape each other. We propose ULME, a post-training framework that unifies embedding and language modeling on a causal decoder-only LLM through predictive coupling. ULME inserts a chain of learnable slots between the query and the answer, reads a semantic embedding and a reasoning embedding from two designated slots, and trains three objectives on a shared causal backbone: a contrastive loss anchoring the semantic slot to document representations, a language-modeling loss supervising answer generation, and a stop-gradient predictive loss that aligns the reasoning slot with a pooled summary of the answer, so that generation actively shapes what the embedding encodes. Across two model families (Qwen2.5, Qwen3) and eight scales spanning 0.5B-8B parameters, a single ULME checkpoint improves MTEB by 3.3-4.1 points over the strongest open-source baseline, tightens BRIGHT and AdvBench-IR simultaneously, and remains on par with a generation-only fine-tune on standard accuracy benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K, MMLU-Pro, HumanEval), demonstrating that genuine unification yields mutually reinforcing rather than merely coexisting capabilities.

Meaning Contracts: Local Semantic Geometry in Large Language Models

Andr\'es Daniel Godoy Ortiz

cs.CL, cs.AI, https://github.com/adgodoyo/Meaning_Contracts

This paper studies the local geometry of semantic meaning in large language models. Current representation analyses ask whether meanings are close in embedding space, or whether linear probes separate semantic classes, but they do not characterize the within-concept local geometry: what is invariant across realizations of a single semantic factor, what varies, and what changes when meaning is refined. This paper characterizes that geometry through two controlled probes evaluated on four architecturally diverse decoder-only LLMs (Gemma 4, Llama 3.2, Qwen 2.5, Pythia). A cross-lingual probe uses language as a perturbation to separate exact meaning matches, same-factor realizations, nearby concepts, and out-of-domain contrasts. A refinement probe separates specificity-bearing semantic narrowing from high-probability attributive elaboration and adversarial controls; both probes are validated by blind human judgments. Four findings recur across all models: under mean pooling, once dominant variance directions are removed, sentence pairs order by semantic relatedness (same factor, then same domain, then different domain) without overlap over a band of layers; semantic factors organize around an invariant core surrounded by structured realization-specific variation; refinement produces directed displacement into tighter neighborhoods, including against controls built on the same base noun; and sentence-level and token-local readouts expose complementary geometry (volume contraction at the sentence level, sharper anisotropic endpoints at token-local readout). We interpret these results as evidence that LLM semantics are organized as local anisotropic neighborhoods in which meaning contracts under refinement.

UniGuard: Multi-View Guardrail Learning for Cross-Interface Safety Generalization

Viet-Thanh Pham, Thuy-Trang Vu, Junae Kim, Rollin Omari, Dinh Phung, Gholamreza Haffari, Lizhen Qu

cs.CL

Guardrail models are increasingly used as safety layers for LLMs and agentic systems, but most are trained on fixed prompt or response interfaces and become unreliable when the same safety case appears as a conversation, agent trajectory, multi-agent trace, or policy-conditioned input. We propose UniGuard, a training framework that learns interface-invariant safety representations from multiple semantically aligned views of the same safety case. We also construct the UniGuard-FT dataset by transforming 1,000 public safety cases into prompt, response, multi-turn conversation, agent-trace, and multi-agent-trace views, and train UniGuard-0.6B with classification, supervised contrastive learning, and asymmetric confidence calibration losses. Across unseen-interface benchmarks ATBench, PolicyGuardBench, and DynaBench, UniGuard-0.6B improves over its pretrained guardrail by 45.12 F1 points on average and reduces expected calibration error by 35.41 points, while remaining competitive with much larger guardrails such as Claude-Sonnet-4.5 and GPT-5.2 - despite being a 0.6B model and trained only with synthetic data.

Where meanings converge and diverge: Revisiting linguistic relativity across 81 languages

Weiran Wang, Wenjuan Qin, Haitao Liu

cs.CL

Do people who speak different languages organize concepts in similar ways? This question lies at the heart of the Sapir--Whorf hypothesis and debates about linguistic relativity. Shared basic meanings, however, need not imply shared relations among them. We compared relations among 196 basic concepts across 81 languages, using word embeddings from two multilingual models to quantify conceptual structure expressed in language. Cross-language correspondence was greater for sociocultural meanings than for meanings concerning nature and motion, a contrast recurring across models and classifications. Within the sociocultural domain, relational differences increased with geographic separation; less closely related languages differed more across all domains. These findings suggest that universality and relativity are interwoven across conceptual domains. They inform an account in which shared cognitive resources interact with linguistic and local experience, and predict greater cross-language correspondence in human semantic judgments for sociocultural than for nature and motion meanings.

Invisibilization as a Source of Probability Bias in Language Models

Christian Arnold, Ernesto Calvo, Guadalupe Gonzalez, Sebasti\'an Vallejo Vera, Tiago Ventura

cs.CL, cs.CY

Language models are aligned to avoid using protected attributes such as race, ethnicity, and gender to inform their responses. Therefore, they increasingly refuse or hedge when such attributes appear in a prompt. However, if a system must decide how much to learn from what people tell it, it is costly to refuse valuable information because of its source. In this article, we measure the cost of declining information in a two-urn game with an exact Bayesian answer. We present leading LLM models from two developers (OpenAI and Anthropic) with a prompt that requires them to decide how far to trust an unverifiable report from a participant whose description we randomize across ten configurations (6,600 sessions). We show that leading models often decline to assess the reporter, treating information as worthless at rates set by the description rather than by the evidence. At its widest, models decline a Black-described reporter 46% of the time and a white-described reporter 9%; yet, when they do assess the information, the Black-described reporter receives slightly more trust. The bias is invisible to audits that compare average trust or look only at assessed cases. It grows with reasoning effort, follows words rather than categories (models invoke race for "Black" but not for its Spanish translation negro, and never for "Indigenous"), and worsens under warning instructions a practitioner would try first. We also present a scoring option that reduces the refusal rate bias. When a model's trust is scored against revealed truth over repeated rounds, it credits identical records equally. In four of five configurations from Anthropic and OpenAI, the racial gap in trust falls below 0.02 by the last rounds.

TutorCaseBench: A Case-Grounded Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Tutors in Supporting Students with Challenging Behaviors

Yeon Su Park, Chanyoung Park, Tae Soo Kim, Seungju Kim, Hyunwoo Kim, Daho Jung, Jinsung Yoon, Juho Kim

cs.CL, cs.HC

LLMs are increasingly used as AI tutors, yet existing benchmarks overlook the challenging behaviors common among students with learning and behavioral difficulties. We introduce TutorCaseBench, a benchmark that grounds AI-tutor evaluation in qualitative case studies from pedagogy, educational psychology, and special education. Our pipeline converts documented student categories into case-specific scenarios, tutor-student dialogues, and rubrics grounded in source evidence. The benchmark contains 923 dialogues across five challenge types, evaluated through open-ended response generation (1,560 items) and multiple-choice selection (2,537 items). Across 11 state-of-the-art LLMs, even the strongest model satisfies a rubric criterion in only 55.8% of open-ended cases, while models are better at selecting appropriate strategies than generating situated responses. These results reveal a gap between intervention recognition and context-specific adaptation, especially for open-weight models. All data are publicly available at https://github.com/kixlab/TutorCaseBench.

TRIAD: Benchmarking Omni-Modal Ambiguity in Multimodal Large Language Models

Zhaolu Kang, Yidi Wang, Yile Li, Siqi Zeng, Yingjie He, Richeng Xuan, Zhichao hu

cs.CL

Ambiguity is a central feature of natural communication: a sentence, image, or sound may remain underspecified until evidence from the other channels is considered. Yet current multimodal benchmarks mostly test perception, recognition, or reasoning over already determinate inputs, leaving unclear whether omni-modal large language models can resolve ambiguity distributed across text, vision, and audio. We introduce TRIAD, a benchmark for omni-modal ambiguity resolution. Each item is a text--image--audio triplet paired with a question and a set of answer options, and is constructed so that the full triplet determines a unique answer while removing any one modality makes the set of consistent options non-unique. TRIAD contains $327$ hand-written queries in $86$ scene groups and covers $18$ ambiguity categories across the three modalities. Evaluating $14$ omni-modal systems reveals that strong item-level performance does not translate into robust scene-level disambiguation: the best model still trails humans by over $20$ points in group accuracy. Removing answer options widens the gap further, driving model group accuracy to the single digits while humans remain robust. Leave-one-out and transcript/description counterfactuals further show that current systems often underuse audio cues when text and image are present. TRIAD reframes omni-modal evaluation around disambiguation rather than recognition, and provides a taxonomy, benchmark, and diagnostic protocol for measuring this capability.

Synthetic-to-Real Code-Switched ASR: A Spontaneous Nepali-English Podcast Benchmark

Firoj Paudel

cs.CL, cs.SD, https://github.com/Firoj-Wise/nepali-english-codeswitch-asr,, Dataset:, https://huggingface.co/datasets/SamaFiroz/nepali-english-codeswitch-podcasts

Nepali-English code-switched conversational speech presents severe challenges for automatic speech recognition (ASR), chiefly through script confusion (inappropriate mixing of Devanagari and Latin orthography) and elevated error rates at language switch points. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) can adapt multilingual foundation models, but human code-switched audio is expensive to collect and annotate. We curate a new benchmark of 13,044 spontaneous podcast utterances (15.45 h total; 10,214 test, 2,830 dev), audited and verified to eliminate machine translation leakages and acoustic loops. We investigate a two-stage synthetic-to-real adaptation framework: Stage 1 applies SFT on 0.95 h of speech synthesized via a multi-speaker, dual-engine TTS pipeline; Stage 2 explores Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with transcription and script rewards. We find that synthetic SFT provides the primary cross-domain transfer to spontaneous human speech, reducing Mixed Error Rate (MER) from 23.40% to 21.52% (-1.88 pp) and switch-point error rate (SPER) from 35.89% to 28.77% (-7.12 pp). Subsequent GRPO yields small, statistically significant adjustments on 173 test utterances (1.69%), driving English script confusion (SCR_en) from 6.45% to 6.36% (p <= 0.001) and SPER to 28.70% (p = 0.022 clustered). Crucially, ablation reveals that in this SFT-converged encoder-decoder setting, an auxiliary script reward term yields performance identical to MER-only optimization because rollout candidate groups exhibit near-zero script variance on synthetic data. On monolingual benchmarks, SFT improves Nepali MER by 2.71 pp (stabilizing at 18.42% after GRPO, a 2.38 pp gain over baseline) while preserving English lexical WER (3.38% vs. 3.33%). Verified transcripts, timestamps, episode metadata, and reconstruction scripts are publicly released.

From Perpetrators' Digital Footprints to Behavioral Communities: An Ontology-Driven Framework for Bengali Cyberbullying Analysis--- FULL VERSION

Md. Nesarul Hoque, Md Hanif Seddiqui, Miskatul Anwara, Rudra Pratap Deb Nath

cs.CL

Cyberbullying involves behavioral and contextual patterns beyond isolated abusive messages. However, existing approaches remain fragmented across content detection, semantic representation, and network analysis, with limited interpretable perpetrator representations, multi-scale structural modeling, and explanations of behavior-driven similarity and influence propagation. These limitations hinder the identification of persistent perpetrators, characterization of behavioral communities, and assessment of potentially influential users. This paper proposes Ontology-Driven Cyberbullying Behavior and Network Analysis (ODCBNA), a perpetrator-centric framework integrating requirements-driven ontology design, knowledge graph construction, behavioral modeling, community analysis, and influence modeling. Using the Bengali Contextual and Diverse Cyberbullying (BenCoDiC) corpus as a use case, ODCBNA identifies $1{,}063$ users with at least three bullying comments or replies as perpetrators and represents each through a $64$-dimensional behavioral and contextual feature vector. Cosine similarity and $k$-nearest-neighbor modeling produce a weighted network with $27{,}554$ edges. Louvain analysis identifies six communities with a modularity of approximately $0.495$ and $27$ sub-communities. Feature-contribution analysis identifies the Video, Impact, and User Profile groups as the strongest contributors to behavioral similarity and community separation, while contextual dimensions support interpretation. Community-constrained influence analysis shows that IM Greedy achieves small positive gains in all six communities, although different seed-selection methods produce comparable diffusion spreads despite limited seed overlap. These findings provide an interpretable basis for understanding perpetrator behavior, community organization, and potential influence patterns in Bengali online environments.

Silicon Samples Match the Answers but Not the Respondents

Yusuke Kumagae, Masahiro Kaneko

cs.CL, cs.CY

Silicon sampling generates survey respondents by having a large language model (LLM) answer a survey as a person with given demographic attributes, and the resulting synthetic populations are increasingly used in place of human ones. Existing evaluations focus on individual-answer accuracy, per-question answer distributions, and correlations across questions. None of these compares the respondents themselves, so a generator whose respondents have combinations of answers found in no human respondent can still score well if it reproduces every answer distribution. We propose the respondent coverage gap (RCG), which represents each respondent as a point defined by their complete answer profile and measures how far each human respondent is from the nearest generated one, relative to the distance between human samples. On two US social surveys, all six LLMs we evaluate leave human respondents uncovered, and under our main settings, they fall behind a baseline that samples each answer independently from the human marginals. Across the prompting and inference settings we test, every LLM has a higher RCG than a Gaussian copula fitted to the human data.

ReflectPO: Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

Rumei Li, Xunliang Cai

cs.CL

With external tools, large language models (LLMs) can tackle complex tasks through sustained environmental interaction. LLM-based agents are therefore increasingly used to tackle complex long-horizon tasks in real-world environments. However, these tasks involve sequential, interdependent decisions and changes in environmental states. Early execution errors affect subsequent interaction histories and environmental states, and their effects accumulate along the decision chain. To address this challenge, we propose Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization (ReflectPO), a reinforcement learning framework for autonomous error recovery in long-horizon tasks. ReflectPO comprises two core components. The first is Reflection-Guided Sampling. We use branch differences and repeated visits in interaction trajectories to construct hints for error recovery. The second is Tree-Structured Credit Assignment. We organize sampled trajectories into trees and assign action-level credit using parent--child reward differences and recovery gains. Experiments over two long-horizon tool-use benchmarks show that ReflectPO outperforms multiple existing baselines in most domains. It also achieves the best overall performance on average. Further analyses show that ReflectPO effectively improves agents' ability to recognize their own deviations, recover from erroneous states, and continue making progress toward task completion.

Verbalizing Activation Differences with Natural Language Autoencoders

Andrea Blasi N\'u\~nez, Gianluca Barmina, Federico Torrielli, Thiusius Rajeeth Savarimuthu, Peter Schneider-Kamp, Lukas Galke Poech

cs.CL

Activation verbalizers translate the internal representations of large language models into human-readable text. The recently proposed natural language autoencoders (NLAs) learn this by reconstructing activations through a natural language bottleneck. However, it remains unclear how well these methods generalize beyond the setting they were trained for. Here, we study this question for NLAs along four closely related questions: (i) Do NLA verbalizations exhibit compositional structure with respect to their input? (ii) Do NLAs generalize to finetuned versions of the model they were trained for? (iii) Can NLAs recover finetuning objectives in model diffing? (iv) Can NLAs be specialized for model diffing? Across four models from three families, our experiments provide evidence supporting a largely positive answer to all four questions. Specifically, verbalizations of composed analogy activations mostly name the target while rarely mentioning the subtracted word, and an NLA can be reused on lightweight finetunes of its original model without retraining, with reconstruction quality degrading noticeably only for the largest model. Verbalizing activation differences between Taboo model organisms and their original models recovers the concealed secret word for most organisms, and reinforcement learning on activation differences further improves this recovery on held-out organisms. We also find that NLAs can recover the finetuning objective without the finetuned model generating any token, since the most informative token positions precede the user prompt. NLAs do not yet match state-of-the-art model diffing methods on the emergent misalignment detection task, but outperform a blackbox baseline. Our findings suggest that NLAs can meaningfully verbalize differences in activation space, rendering NLAs a promising direction for future work.

RetiJev: Fast and Source-Grounded Ophthalmic Decision Models

Bo Liu, Lianyu Wang, Meng Wang, Ching-Yu Cheng, Huazhu Fu

cs.CL, cs.AI

Ophthalmic clinical decision support requires fast, accurate, and privacy-preserving inference, yet autoregressive large language models (LLMs) incur substantial latency and computational overhead for decisions among predefined actions. We therefore investigate a decision-oriented alternative to generative reasoning. Specifically, we first introduce RetiJev-Data, a source-grounded ophthalmology corpus comprising 78,158 clinical-decision and diagnosis questions derived from articles in PubMed Central and a MeSH vocabulary covering 531 eye diseases, and 50,074 guideline-grading questions spanning 11 grading systems, along with a 24,662-question evaluation benchmark. Based on this, we develop RetiJev, a family of 0.8B-9B decision models that directly score candidate answers without autoregressive decoding. Across all model sizes, RetiJev outperforms both a 27B LLM and the commercial Jev 1.13 decision model on guideline grading by 10.4-23.0 points. RetiJev-9B further achieves the highest accuracy on clinical decisions, improving over both baselines by 5.4-6.5 points while matching them on diagnosis. It answers a case in 158 ms on one GPU, 3.5x faster than LLM generation and 470x faster than reasoning. Models are released at https://github.com/Awenbocc/RetiJev.

IQuest-Q1: Advancing Foundation Capabilities for Agentic CLI Systems

A. Jian Yang, An Huang, Bo Dan, Chen Mu, Chengran Yang, Chuan Shi, Feng Chang, Hongxin Li, Hongyuan Tao, Jinxing Zhang, Jujie He, Kun Wang, Lin Jing, Ming Yang, Qicheng Hu, Renyuan Li, Shark Liu, Shawn Guo, Shi Qing, Shi Zhong, Tianyu Zheng, Wei Liu, Wei Zhang, Wenrui Cai, Wenxiao Chen, Xuxing Chen, Yan Xing, Yi Xing, Yifan Luo, Yiran Xing, Yizhi Li, Yongsheng Kang, Yucheng Ding, Yue Song, Yun Qing, Yunqi Liu, Zelong Huang, Zhengmao Ye, Zhentao Fan, Zhiteng Li, Ziling Huang, Bihong Tang, Bingli Wang, Ce Jun, Changhao Chen, Chen Hu, Chen Xiao, Chi Liu, Chunjiang Ge, Danyang Li, Deyang Kong, Dian Qi, Ding Tan, Evan Yang, Fanglin Xu, Fei Bai, Gang Liu, Guochang Li, Hao Tang, Haoran Feng, Haowen Wang, Huahao Hu, Huanjin Yao, Huatong Song, Jia Deng, Jiajun Wu, Jialiang Cheng, Jiayi Liang, Jiayi Wu, et al. (108 additional authors not shown)

cs.CL

We present IQuest-Q1, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) agentic foundation model with 320B total parameters (15B activated). IQuest-Q1 is developed through a three-stage process: (1) stable and efficient pre-training, combining Stable SplitNorm, Muon, and muP with a two-stage curriculum whose second stage increases the coding-data mixture, preserving a wide stability margin between safe and optimal learning rates; (2) fine-grained mid-training, expanding the data mixture across domains and formats and progressively extending the context; and (3) multi-scaffold with multi-version post-training, combining SFT, domain-specialized RL, and MOPD across various scaffolds to integrate complementary training signals and strengthen agentic coding capabilities. Furthermore, model merging combines different experts and checkpoints from different stages while mitigating conflicts among their parameter updates. The resulting IQuest-Q1 demonstrates capabilities in both coding and general tasks. We release IQuest-Q1 as an open-source model to support community research and development.

Once a Tree, Always a Tree? Cross-lingual Transfer of Hierarchical Generalization in Language Models

Shin Yamaguchi, Masaya Taniguchi, Tatsuki Kuribayashi, Taro Watanabe, Yoshinobu Kano

cs.CL

Language models (LMs) often exhibit successful cross-lingual transfer through continued (pre)training, still their underlying process remains unclear. One possibility is that LMs can leverage the evidence toward language-universal principles, namely, hierarchical structure, from a source language (L1) to a new language (L2), while many other non-linguistic explanations, e.g., improved initialization of position embeddings, can be potentially possible. In this study, to quantify the cross-linguistic transferability of linguistic evidence in cross-lingual LM training, we introduce a new experimental design, namely, a cross-lingual poverty-of-stimulus (CPOS) setting that ablates how LMs' hierarchical generalization in L2 can be led by structural evidence provided in L1. We contrast counterfactual L1 and L2 learning scenarios where the L2 corpus is ambiguous between linear and hierarchical grammar rules, while the amount of positive evidence for the hierarchical rule is controlled on the L1 side. Our experimental results show that LMs can partially, yet not perfectly, transfer L1 structural evidence to L2, yielding only a small but detectable hierarchical bias, while the strength of transfer depends on language pairs. We further ablate the conditions for successful transfer, motivated by recent reports on the substantial impact of vocabulary overlap. We indeed find that vocabulary overlap is crucial even for the cross-lingual transfer of hierarchical syntactic generalization. This suggests that this also explains the language-pair-dependent tendencies in the main experiments and suggests that the transfer of linguistic evidence in continued new-language learning is only conditionally achievable.

Joint or Per-Option? Compact Encoders for Closed-Set Decisions from 17 Million to 1 Billion Parameters

Tardelli Ronan Coelho Stekel

cs.CL, cs.LG, https://github.com/tardellirs/crivo-decision-encoders, ;, models:, https://huggingface.co/tardellirs

Many language-model pipelines end in a closed-set decision, such as selecting a tool. Prompting a large language model for it is slow and order-sensitive. We ask how small an encoder for such decisions can be and whether it should read the options jointly or separately. In a three-seed study at 32 million parameters, per-option scoring beats joint scoring on a held-out sample of a 37-benchmark decision suite by 5.8 points of chance-corrected skill at matched optimizer steps and by 2.8 at approximately matched training compute; two external benchmarks agree, though not significantly on their unseen tasks. Crossing reader with initialisation shows that the advantage requires a reranker initialisation, whose query-candidate pair input matches per-option reading; from the raw encoder the two readers are indistinguishable. Per-option scoring needs 5 to 13 times more encoder passes: at most twice the median latency, but more than four times the mean. Of six released joint encoders (17 million to 1 billion parameters), the 68- and 150-million-parameter models exceed a 435-million-parameter DeBERTa-v3 zero-shot classifier by 4.9 and 5.9 accuracy points on 209 overlap-audited System1-Arena tasks and are at least on par with it on the 145 tasks unseen in training. The smallest answers a median request in 14 milliseconds on 16 processor threads, against 728 for the classifier. Reordering options changes 12 to 23% of the joint models' answers (per-option scoring: none). Up to 68 million parameters, they lead their size range on a public leaderboard's private, mainly in-distribution tests.

A Reality Check of Inference-Time Context Augmentation Methods

Haeun Yu, Geert Heyman, Pepa Atanasova, Isabelle Augenstein

cs.CL

Large language models (LLMs) often fail to utilise the context given in the input, resulting in hallucinations or a fallback to parametric knowledge. Context augmentation methods aim to improve model performance by modifying the input, from inserting filler tokens that add computational time to adding relevant content such as elaboration of a context during inference. However, these methods have been evaluated in isolation and only by their performance gains, leaving it unclear how they compare and whether they reliably improve performance. In this paper, we present an evaluation framework spanning five methods that differ in how informative the inserted content is and apply them at the sentence level across seven models spanning multiple sizes on three datasets. We find that accuracy gains are not always genuine improvements but undirected perturbations: the methods correct some predictions while breaking others, particularly when the inserted text allows only for more computational time. Our analysis reveals that methods that bring no additional content primarily change model predictions near the decision boundary. Our findings highlight the need to evaluate the reliability of the accuracy gains before adopting them in practice.

Interpretive Variation and Discourse Structure in Detecting Enthymemes in Controversial Political Discourse

Martial Pastor, Nelleke Oostdijk

cs.CL

Enthymemes are arguments that leave a premise or conclusion unstated, and they are a common persuasion device on social media. Because readers must fill in the missing part themselves, they often disagree on whether a text contains one. This paper asks how to detect and evaluate enthymemes when such disagreement is part of the task. On tweets about vaccination and immigration, each labeled by five annotators, F1 stalls around 0.67 for every fine-tuned model we train, regardless of data size, added features or label type. We therefore ask how these models should be evaluated instead, and propose metrics that compare predictions with the full distribution of annotator votes, together with a model that predicts this distribution directly and captures disagreement better at no cost in F1. We then ask what role discourse structure plays. Models learn to rely on explicit argumentative structure: they reliably recognize tweets without an enthymeme but rarely commit on tweets that contain one. Both the models and a discourse parser miss arguments whose links are left implicit and must be inferred by the reader. Finally, we discuss what these blind spots mean for content moderation systems trained on majority labels.

Padding to k: A Minimum Count in the Schema Makes Open-Weight Models Pad Their Extraction Lists

Abhishek Barali, Aayush Paudel

cs.CL, data:, https://github.com/AbhishekBarali/padding-to-k

Extraction pipelines ask language models for JSON that follows a schema, and the schema can set a minimum number of list entries. We ask what models do when the document holds fewer items than that minimum. Under a preregistered plan, we test eight open-weight models on 558 real documents from nine annotated datasets, with six more models on hosted services as a reference. Three findings stand out. First, models treat the count as a target: a minimum shown in the schema, but not enforced, raises the share of lists longer than the true count from 34.5% to 55.4%. Second, the padding is hard to catch, because most added entries are real text taken from the document; about half of them pass a check that each entry occurs in the source. Third, common fixes help only partly: a line saying fewer items are fine removes the effect on average but misses more true items, a count in prose ("up to k") also raises over-counting, an "N/A" option under an enforced minimum still leaves half the lists padded, and re-asking with the validation error makes padding far more common. We release the code, answer lists and run records at https://github.com/AbhishekBarali/padding-to-k

Mostly Reading: What Open-Book Scores Measure in Document-Level LoRA Adapters

Martino M. L. Pulici, Cuong Xuan Chu, Evgeny Kharlamov, Volker Tresp

cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG

Single-document LoRA adapters have been proposed as units of knowledge to be merged and retrieved; yet open-book scoring, with the document in the prompt, can credit a document's own adapter (its specialist) for reading that document as readily as for holding it. Training one adapter for each of 150 Wikipedia articles on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, we find that open-book scores mostly measure a reading behaviour every adapter shares: a random adapter trained on another document recovers 82 % of the specialist's open-book advantage over the base model but only 2 % of its advantage on a cloze probe that asks for the same document's facts without providing the source text, a share we cannot distinguish from zero. Content does reach the weights, since the specialists beat the base model in cloze recall by 0.094, and the geometry of their updates follows the documents they read; yet, in a test of 30 queries, adapters chosen by weight-space proximity or text embedding are not distinguishable from random ones on either probe, although that test cannot exclude an advantage of about four fifths of what a query's own adapter gains over a random one. At 16 documents or more, correctly scaled merges recover 87 % to 89 % of the specialists' open-book advantage but only 12 % to 16 % of their cloze advantage, with intervals that run from below zero to at most 32 %, against 94 % for joint training.We also report four silent errors, each of which produced a clean, wrong result.

Chunk Recomputation under Real Document Revisions: Measuring Update Cost and Retrieval Quality of Fixed, Recursive, and Content-Defined Chunking

Yuning Bian, Lele Xu, Yifan Yuan, Xinyu Yin, Chengyan Zhan

cs.CL, cs.IR

Chunking strategies for retrieval-augmented generation are almost always evaluated on a static snapshot, yet the documents underlying a knowledge base continue to change. Because chunks are embedded independently, a boundary that moves after an edit forces unchanged text to be re-embedded. We measure this update cost on 120 pairs of adjacent Chinese and English Wikipedia revisions for three stateless chunkers (non-overlapping fixed-size, recursive paragraph-first, and content-defined chunking) under a strict byte-identical reuse rule and a frozen dense encoder. On the 116-article common-success pool, with frozen, length-calibrated parameters, fixed-size chunking recomputes 57-68% of its full new-version embedding-input workload, recursive chunking 38-56%, and content-defined chunking 43-54% (it failed on 4 of 62 Chinese articles), while at these parameters mean evidence recall under a fixed 2048-token retrieval budget differs by less than two percentage points within either language. A cost-only parameter grid shows that the ranking of the two content-aware chunkers changes with chunk-length settings, whereas non-overlapping fixed-size chunking has the highest recomputation cost in every length-matched condition. Controlled edits and a verbatim-presence diagnostic support boundary drift as one contributor to recomputation of text already present in the previous version. Update cost is therefore a substantial, parameter-sensitive dimension that static retrieval evaluations do not expose and that should be reported alongside retrieval quality.

Harm Is Behavioural, Moderation Is Lexical: How Language and Culture Decide Whom Automated Safety Systems Protect and Whom They Silence

Gabriel Lars Sabadin, Dr Nicola Harding, Sarah Kingston

cs.CL, cs.AI, https://osf.io/v7aef/files/osfstorage?view_only=9449667241894b1ca7d336fc4b8259d8

Content warning: this paper quotes slurs and hateful statements from published test suites; the racial slur among them is masked. Automated systems decide at scale which messages are harmful, and they decide unevenly. Classifiers label African American English as offensive up to twice as often as other English, the accounts of prominent drag queens can score as more toxic than those of white nationalists, and safety behaviour that holds in English collapses in low-resource languages. We argue that over-flagging and under-protection share one cause: deciding harm from vocabulary and surface form rather than from what a message does. We organise the evidence into seven failure modes and argue, as testable hypotheses, that harmful behaviours (a request for secrecy, a demand for payment, an attempt to isolate someone) travel across languages better than the words that express them. We propose an evaluation protocol that makes the gap between the best- and worst-served language communities a headline result, and show that the naive gap is inflated under equal service and that its usual bootstrap interval is unreliable. Applied to five open systems in eleven languages, from a keyword lexicon to two current multilingual guard models, the protocol finds that none reaches native-speaker accuracy at separating harmful function from harmless form (51 of 55 system-language pairs fall short); that greater capability raises discrimination in nine languages while leaving Arabic and romanised Hindi near chance, so the gap between the best- and worst-served languages grows with capability; that one system both over-flags and under-protects the same language most; and that a language a system reads worst can look the most fairly treated when only false positives are reported.

What Pairwise Evaluation Misses: Reward Scores across Prompts

Mateusz Modrzejewski, Tomasz Radzikowski

cs.CL, cs.AI

Reward models score responses for preference optimization, filtering and ranking, and pooling scores across prompts needs them to be comparable. Yet pairwise evaluation compares two responses to the same prompt, so any score component shared by a prompt's responses cancels and goes untested. We study this component by comparing between-prompt variation in model scores with the same variation in human ratings of the same responses. We propose a human-referenced audit, built from a between-prompt variance share, a sibling-rating regression and prompt substitutions, and apply it to eighteen released scorers and six matched training conditions. We find that scores cluster by prompt more than the ratings do: on HelpSteer2, six reward models have between-prompt shares of 0.34 to 0.64 against a human share near zero.
