Existing graph, diffusion, and flow-based methods typically impose a fixed structural bias, which can oversmooth abrupt changes or propagate errors from unreliable neighbors. We introduce CTRL-FM, an observation-preserving flow-matching framework that treats structural guidance as an outage-conditioned inference-time decision. A missing-only flow-matching backbone learns a conditional velocity field while keeping observed measurements exactly fixed. For each realized outage, CTRL-FM constructs self-supervised proxy outages from surviving measurements and uses their known values to select among uncontrolled, graph-dominant, temporal-dominant, and balanced control policies. The selected policy is locally modulated by graph and temporal reliability estimated exclusively from surviving observations, while boundary-anchored trends provide structural guidance. Control is applied only to missing entries and is rejected when proxy evidence does not sufficiently improve over uncontrolled sampling. Across PeMS08, METR-LA, Air-36, and AQI, CTRL-FM achieves the strongest overall performance under 30% and 60% node outages. Stress tests with up to 75% sensor loss further show graceful degradation and consistent gains over training-time and fixed structural control. These results demonstrate that validating, selecting, and locally adapting structural guidance for each realized outage is more effective than imposing a single global structural prior.

Probing and Steering for Safety-Relevant Behaviours in Large Language Models

Nimun Bajwa, Massimo Poesio

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CY, interface:, https://github.com/NimunB/probing-safety-behaviours

Activation probes are computed directions in a language model's residual stream, used both to detect safety-relevant behaviours and, by adding their direction back into the stream, to steer them. We explore how the main design choices behind such probes interact, within one pipeline, on one model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct), and across three behaviours: deception, high-stakes awareness and sycophancy. The pipeline crosses two estimators (difference-of-means and logistic regression) with three token-pooling schemes (last, mean and learned attention), selects appropriate layers to detect or steer a behaviour, and evaluates every direction twice: as a detector of the behaviour, and as a steering vector to push outputs towards the behaviour. Prior work read together suggests a read/write dissociation, in which logistic regression reads (detects) best but difference-of-means directions write (steer) best. We find that it holds for deception and high-stakes but not for sycophancy, where the pattern reverses. The reversal appears to follow partially from the data rather than from the estimators. The best pooling technique and the layer choice depend on the behaviour and data, and the layer that steers best can sit well before the layer that detects best. The right steering metric to use between changing a model's perception and eliciting behaviour depends on the task - and the two can disagree outright. The exported probes and directions power an interactive interface through which a user can score and steer text directly.

Bayesian Fixed-Confidence Best-Arm Identification with Abstention

Yuqi Huang, Yunlong Hou, Vincent Y. F. Tan

cs.LG

We study the Bayesian fixed-confidence best-arm identification problem in which a learner can abstain from making a terminal recommendation. Subject to a target error probability $\delta$ and an abstention budget $\alpha$, we propose Screen-and-Confirm (S&C) and analyze its expected stopping time. We further derive the minimum expected stopping time for any algorithm that satisfies these constraints. For Gaussian priors and rewards, as $(\delta,\alpha)\downarrow(0,0)$, we establish matching upper and lower bounds up to multiplicative constants, with rate $\kappa_\nu^2[1+\log((\alpha+\delta)/\delta)]/(\alpha+\delta)$, where $\kappa_\nu$ is the prior density of the top-two gap at zero, highlighting that nearly tied instances govern the sampling cost. When abstention is disallowed ($\alpha=0$), S&C yields the optimal $\Theta(1/\delta)$ dependence for fixed model parameters, removing the additional $\log(1/\delta)$ factor in the upper bound of Jang et al. (2024) under our Gaussian assumptions. We further characterize the exact multiplicative constant of the minimum expected stopping time for practical regimes including (i) fixed $\alpha \in (0,1)$ and (ii) $\alpha/\delta\to\infty$, which are also achieved by S&C.

Escaping the Phylogenetic Trap: Persistent Homology Enables Cross-Species Ion Channel Classification via Multimodal Graph Neural Networks

Arezoo Bybordi, Favour Achimba, Mariam Gelashvili, Jessy Ramirez, Weigang Qiu, Mand\"e Holford, Anita Raja

cs.LG, q-bio.BM

Protein classifiers are highly sensitive to evaluation protocol: random train/test splits induce phylogenetic leakage, allowing models to exploit taxon-specific correlations and substantially overestimate out-of-distribution performance. This issue is particularly acute for venom compound classification by ion-channel target (Ca2+, K+, Na+), where reported accuracies of 80-90% do not reflect generalization to unseen taxa. We formulate cross-species prediction as a structured distribution shift in which taxonomic identity acts as a confounder, requiring representations that capture structural features invariant across phylogeny. Using a shared multimodal backbone that fuses ESM-2 sequence embeddings with a disulfide-aware graph attention network, we systematically compare five structural inductive biases (spectral, Riemannian, SE(3)-invariant, statistical-manifold, and persistent-homology representations) under a leave-one-taxon-out evaluation protocol on a benchmark of 1,373 ColabFold-predicted venom compounds spanning five taxa. The persistent-homology representation summarizes backbone geometry through topological descriptors that are robust to local structural perturbations, providing an inductive bias toward phylogenetically invariant learning. Under conventional i.i.d. splits, all models achieve similar accuracy (81-86%), masking differences in inductive bias. Under taxonomic shift, this parity collapses: a sequence-only baseline drops to approximately 30%, the remaining geometric priors cluster at 54-55%, while our persistent-homology model (PH-GNN) achieves 63.4%, outperforming the next-best method by over 8 percentage points. These results demonstrate that random-split evaluation obscures meaningful differences in representation quality and identify topological representations as an effective inductive bias for learning under structured biological distribution shift.

AcoustiPrint: Physics-Guided Neural Design of Manufacturable Acoustic Structures

Shanmuka Sadhu, Harshvardhan C. Takawale, Yehia Zakaria, Ahmed Allam, and Nirupam Roy

cs.LG, cs.AI

Recent work in additive manufacturing and neural inverse design enables the creation of complex three-dimensional structures to manipulate physical fields, yet these designs must also adhere to discrete, non-differentiable fabrication constraints. Current methods limit the design space by imposing differentiable soft manufacturability loss penalties, or by fixing structures through post-processing, causing a discrepancy between the geometry optimized by the physics model and the final fabricated result. Thus, we present AcoustiPrint, a physics-informed neural architecture for constrained inverse design that represents three-dimensional geometries with an implicit neural representation (INR) and passes each candidate through a printability operator before evaluating its final response. The printability operator executes layer-wise self-support, discrete material selection, and whole-body connectivity, assuring fabrication feasibility. To allow for backpropagation through the discrete non-differentiable operations, we use surrogate gradients for the backward pass and a self-supervised printability objective that progressively drives the INR toward a valid design domain. We demonstrate AcoustiPrint for personalized transcranial focused ultrasound, where inter-patient differences in acoustic properties and head geometry deform wave propagation and requires patient-specific acoustic focusing. AcoustiPrint jointly optimizes three-dimensional geometry and material composition through differentiable wave physics while ensuring manufacturability. Experiments show manufacturable acoustic structures with enhanced focal accuracy and over density-based and phase-based baselines, demonstrating a generalized methodology for neural design through non-differentiable manufacturing constraints and differentiable physics.

Memory-Augmented Online Test-Time Adaptation for EEG Motor Imagery via Perturbation Consistency and Confidence Calibration

Kunkun Zhang, Kehao Wu, Zhitao Chen, Haigen Hu, Qianwei Zhou

cs.LG, eess.SP, Measurement, (TIM),, 2026

Recently, electroencephalography (EEG)-based brain--computer interfaces (BCIs) have gained traction as portable neural measurement systems. However, the practical deployment of these instruments is often hindered by nonstationarity and cross-subject distribution shifts, which lead to significant intersubject inconsistency. Existing adaptation methods remain impractical for real-time measurement scenarios, as they typically require offline access to source or aggregated target data. To address these challenges, this article proposes memory-augmented online test-time adaptation (OTTA) with perturbation consistency and confidence calibration (MOPC), a source-free framework designed to enhance the reliability of BCI instrumentation. MOPC enables the measurement system to update its decoding parameters on the fly during the inference stage, facilitating single-pass adaptation without accessing sensitive source data. We introduce random-mask perturbations to enforce prediction consistency against inherent EEG signal noise and acquisition artifacts. Furthermore, a confidence-calibrated reweighting loss is designed to integrate feature-space similarity, effectively mitigating unreliable pseudolabels and class imbalance during continuous signal acquisition. To ensure system stability and prevent the catastrophic forgetting of established neural patterns, a lightweight memory module is incorporated to regularize parameter updates within a tight latency budget. Evaluated on the BCI IV IIb and OpenBMI datasets, MOPC achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming prior methods by 2.22~\% and 3.67~\%, respectively, while keeping the per-trial adaptation latency to approximately 40--60 ms. This study provides a robust, recalibration-free solution for real-time EEG decoding, marking a significant advancement in the precision and usability of practical BCI measurement instruments.

Towards Faithful Data Selection: Statistical Limits and Scalable Algorithms

Ahmed Mehdi Inane, Mahdi Haghifam, Gintare Karolina Dziugaite, Ioannis Mitliagkas

cs.LG

What does it mean to have an optimal data selection algorithm? Optimality is conventionally framed as finding a subset that minimizes the population error of the classifier trained only on that subset. Our starting point in this work is first to show that this goal is information-theoretically impossible unless the data selection algorithm has access to a validation set order-wise larger than the training set. This impossibility result motivates shifting from unverifiable population risk minimization to a one-sided guarantee that we refer to as faithfulness: for any candidate subset, we provide an estimable proxy that upper-bounds the difference between training on the full training data and on only the subset. We model training with the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm and bound the Kullback--Leibler divergence between subset-trained and full-data-trained parameter distributions by the trajectory-accumulated gradient discrepancy. Minimizing this discrepancy gives a principled way to select a subset. Even though the resulting problem is NP-hard, we propose a swap-based local search that decreases the bound at every accepted step. It can therefore refine the output of any selector without worsening its bound. Finally, we provide experimental results on CIFAR10 and CIFAR100 showing that it matches existing data selection baselines. Implementation of our algorithm and the code for reproducing the results are available at: https://github.com/Mehdi-Inane/data_selection_swap.

aggmetrics: Aggregating Classification Performance Measures for Machine Learning

Ronaldo Cristiano Prati

cs.LG, cs.AI, https://github.com/rcprati/aggmetrics,, documentation:, https://aggmetrics.readthedocs.io,, archived, at, doi:10.5281/zenodo.23535051

aggmetrics is a Python package that extends any binary performance measure expressed in the four confusion-matrix counts to multiclass, multilabel, ordinal, cost-sensitive, soft-label and cross-validation settings, by substituting one of four aggregation operators for what libraries usually expose as an average switch. The package also stores, for every measure, the structural properties that decide which identities apply to it, can infer those properties from a user-supplied formula, and uses them to warn when a measure is used where it does not behave as commonly assumed. Its test suite encodes the results of the companion theory as executable tests. The package is BSD-3 licensed, depends only on NumPy, and is available at https://github.com/rcprati/aggmetrics; documentation at https://aggmetrics.readthedocs.io.

Short-to-Long: Detached Features for Long-Horizon Value Learning

Tong Che, Boris Ivanovic, Marco Pavone

cs.LG, cs.AI

Learning useful representations from continually changing value targets remains a challenge in deep reinforcement learning. We study the temporal horizon of this supervision: smaller discounts make these targets less sensitive to distant bootstrap changes, but using them for behavior changes the control objective. We introduce Short-to-Long (S2L), which supplies a long-horizon behavior learner with features trained by smaller-discount source networks. Detached connections prevent the behavior value loss from updating the sources, allowing each source to follow its own value objective while the behavior discount remains unchanged. Across Atari, online continuous control, and offline continuous control, the approach yields aggregate performance gains, improving the paired terminal median on 40 of 57 Atari games. Matched Atari ablations support the roles of feature transfer, smaller source discounts, and gradient isolation. Fixed-trajectory measurements further show reduced drift in source value targets with mixed discounts.

DiffLTM: Continuous Diffusions Large Tabular Models for Conditional Generation

Alankrita Bhatt, Mukur Gupta, Andrea Montanari, Pulkit Tandon

cs.LG

Conditional generation is a key capability to be supported by Large Tabular Models, enabling a number of applications. Accurate conditional generation has to contend with two key challenges: $(i)$ Data comprise a mixture of numerical and categorical entries, the latter often with high cardinality and heavily unbalanced proportions; $(ii)$ Conditional generation becomes increasingly difficult for rare sub-populations. The best models in this class use a hybrid of continuous and discrete diffusions. In this paper, we design a continuous diffusion model with strong generation quality. An important element is the use of cross-entropy loss for categoricals, for which we provide a first theoretical justification. We introduce metrics that focus on rare subpopulations. Further, we generalize conditional generation techniques to enable new types of conditioning. Finally, we show that our model (trained on labor data) can capture classical insights from econometrics. Code is available at https://github.com/granica-ai/DiffLTM

Rethinking SAE Evaluation: An Atomic Interpretable Unit Framework Reveals Hidden Polysemanticity and Redundancy

Riteng Zhang, Xiaoqian Wang

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, Code:, https://github.com/ritengzhang77-max/SAE-AIU

Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) were originally motivated by discovering monosemantic features, a property that characterizes individual features but does not specify what the learned feature set should look like as a whole. As SAEs have increasingly been adopted for attribution, circuit discovery, and intervention, researchers have begun evaluating metrics that describe global properties of the feature set, such as sparsity, dead feature rates, reconstruction fidelity, and disentanglement, a diverse set that extends far beyond monosemanticity. However, this implicit shift beyond monosemanticity has not been formalized as a clear, well-defined objective that comes before these evaluation metrics. Without such an objective, existing metrics remain ad hoc, with their necessity unjustified. In this work, we formalize this ultimate objective by introducing a new paradigm for SAE evaluation: requiring that SAE features, and more generally dictionary-based decompositions of model representations, form an atomic interpretable unit basis, a coordinate system for semantically meaningful explanation. Under this paradigm, we organize existing and new measurements such as DAME (Dominance-Aware Monosemanticity Evaluation) as proxy metrics and introduce a comprehensive benchmarking framework. Evaluating hundreds of SAEs across multiple architectures and model families, we uncover failure modes in which some SAEs appear monosemantic under existing evaluation metrics while remaining functionally polysemantic: the most dominant features, those contributing most to model computation, are systematically less interpretable and more redundant. These failure modes warrant attention from SAE researchers as targets for improved training and architectures, and motivate dominance-aware evaluation as a standard check for practitioners before SAE features are deployed for attribution, circuit tracing, or intervention.

Optimal Multitask Representations Under a Rate Constraint: An Information Bottleneck Analysis

Nicholas Stiles, Gregory W. Wornell

cs.LG, cs.IT, math.IT, total

Which pretraining objective yields the best representation for downstream tasks under an information budget? We formalize this problem as a multitask information bottleneck, using a mutual information constraint to model incomplete information retention. For Gaussian inputs and tasks, we characterize optimal representations and show that a weakly task-aligned specialist can outperform a reconstruction generalist at low rates but lose at high rates. We also construct task families for which every fixed Gaussian prediction objective incurs arbitrarily large relative regret, driven by reversals in optimal allocation priorities. Conversely, the average task information matrix defines an objective with a uniform relative-regret bound controlled by maximum task SNR. CelebA and dSprites experiments illustrate that compression can change which pretraining objective performs best, and even task-aware training can reverse which downstream task is better preserved as rate increases.

NEST: Nested Embedding Representation for Multiscale Clustering

Zhanqi Zhang, Mikio Aoi, Gal Mishne

cs.LG

Real-world data often exhibits cluster structure at multiple resolutions, yet existing methods typically learn a single partition or construct an explicit hierarchy. Inspired by Matryoshka Representation Learning, we introduce NEST, an unsupervised framework that encodes coarse-to-fine structure within a single embedding. NEST jointly optimizes clustering and separation across nested prefix subspaces, enabling progressively finer distinctions as embedding dimensionality increases, with rank and within-cluster regularization that keep the embedding from collapsing. Empirically, distances in the learned embedding follow the hierarchy distance more closely than those of the input, although the hierarchy is never observed during training. On synthetic hierarchies, NEST recovers multiscale structure given only the number of clusters per level. Experiments on vision, text, and single-cell data further demonstrate multiscale organization across real-world representations. By making clustering granularity an intrinsic property of the representation, NEST provides a unified approach to multiscale structure discovery.

When News Gets Old: Portfolio Allocation with Reinforcement Learning and Large Language Models

Bolin Song (Cornell University), Surui Xiang (United Nations)

cs.LG, q-fin.PM

Financial news can influence investment decisions long after publication. Timely news may reach an investor before prices fully adjust, whereas older news may already be reflected in prices. We examine two ways to handle aging signals: retain them and give a portfolio policy timing information, or filter them out before portfolio selection. A fixed large language model (LLM) extracts signals from public SEC disclosures. We compare these treatments in reinforcement learning (RL) policies and LLMs that choose portfolios directly. We evaluate returns after trading costs in historical tests over 2023-2025. RL choices respond to timing information in a controlled test, but historical returns show no consistent benefit from adding it. For the LLM, filtering old news produced the highest average net return, reaching 18.43% per year when news delivery was delayed by four weeks. This exceeded the returns of all RL strategies that used news.

Evaluating Type-1 Diabetes Digital Twins by Decision Quality

Victor Li, Owen Tucker, Michael S. Hughes, Temiloluwa Prioleau, and Shengpu Tang

cs.LG, https://github.com/Veeeeeee7/T1D-Digital-Twin-Benchmark

A digital twin (DT) for type 1 diabetes (T1D) can predict how an individual's blood glucose will respond to different insulin treatments without actually trying them. For each treatment, downstream statistics of predicted glucose trajectories, such as glycemic risk, can be computed to guide clinical decisions. However, the quality of DTs is usually measured by the accuracy of their predicted glucose trajectories, which may not reflect the quality of the downstream treatment decisions they inform. In this work, we directly evaluate DTs by this decision quality on downstream treatment ranking, i.e., whether a DT predicts glucose trajectories that can be used to rank candidate treatments as the real patient would. We present an evaluation protocol over 30 virtual patients from the FDA-accepted UVA/Padova model, each simulated under 21 different basal-bolus treatments. For each patient, a DT is fitted under one treatment and then produces glucose trajectories for the remaining 20 treatments. Our results show that prediction accuracy and decision quality disagree. A baseline that models the average patient, never seeing the individual's glucose data, has the lowest prediction accuracy of all six methods (RMSE: 63.6 mg/dL) but ranks treatments with the second-highest mean Spearman (0.766), while a black-box neural network that predicts glucose nearly twice as accurately (RMSE: 32.9 mg/dL) ranks them no better than a random ordering of the 20 treatments (0.049 Spearman). Therefore, selecting a DT based on prediction accuracy alone can prefer one that recommends treatments at chance, and decision quality must be evaluated directly rather than assumed to follow from prediction accuracy.

Future of Time Series Anomaly Detection: From Historical Review to Unified Outlook

Zhijie Zhong, Zhiwen Yu, Pengyu Li, Liujian Zhang, Qiaoli Ni, Jiang Jun, Jianming Lv, Kaixiang Yang, C. L. Philip Chen

cs.LG, cs.AI, Artificial, Intelligence., DOI:, 10.1109/TAI.2026.3746935

Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) is a foundational problem in data mining, aiming to identify observations that deviate significantly from nominal temporal patterns. Driven by escalating data volumes and application complexity, recent research has yielded diverse architectures, including diffusion models, state space models, and large language models (LLMs). However, existing surveys provide limited coverage of these emerging designs and lack a unified treatment of multivariate anomalies, cross-domain unified modeling, metric reliability, and contemporary evaluation challenges. To bridge these gaps, this survey delivers three core contributions: (1) We establish a refined anomaly categorization and a structured taxonomy of paradigms reflecting modern advances, comparing detection mechanisms and charting developmental trajectories across paradigms. (2) We provide an extensive analysis of evaluation methodologies and conduct comprehensive experiments to assess metric fidelity, complemented by an open-source benchmark repository encompassing algorithms, datasets, and evaluation tools. (3) We outline key open challenges, offering forward-looking perspectives on dynamic thresholding, model interpretability, zero-shot transfer, multi-modal alignment, and foundational models for time series. By addressing existing theoretical and empirical limitations, this survey establishes a coherent framework to guide future TSAD research and practical deployments.

Interpreting Model Reasoning through Behavioral Counterfactuals

Shawn Im, James Oldfield, Adel Bibi, Sharon Li

cs.LG, cs.AI

Faithfully understanding the internal process that occurs as models form their chain of thought is an important problem with the rise in model capabilities. However, this remains challenging due to the variance in sampled responses, making it difficult to isolate the effects of interventions and compare model reasoning systematically. We propose a method for localizing and analyzing how a given intervention affects the model's reasoning through behavioral counterfactuals: given a fixed response, how does introducing an intervention to the prompt change the next-token distributions? We train a module to edit the residual stream such that it minimizes the KL divergence between the output distributions for a fixed response under a source prompt and its counterfactual target prompt. We find that matching the output distributions recovers the counterfactual behavior more effectively than standard activation reconstruction objectives and validate that attribution of the trained module outputs is effective for identifying causally important locations for counterfactual recovery. The attribution maps and visualizations of the downstream effects of model edits provide an interface for generating hypotheses about the original model's computation, which can then be tested with activation patching. In a case study on Gemma-4-E4B-it and DeepSeek-R1-0528-Qwen3-8B, we investigate how a suggestion influences model reasoning even when it is not verbally acknowledged. Our analysis identifies representations at the question statement and filler tokens that mediate a substantial part of the suggestion's influence, with downstream effects such as changing the framing of the justification or even the final answer.

RiboBridge: protein-conditioned de novo RNA binder structure design

Zhou Zhang, Hanqun Cao, Minghao Sun, Odin Zhang, Yang Zhang, Pheng Ann Heng, Wujun Li, Tianfan Fu

cs.LG, q-bio.BM

Designing RNA molecules that bind to target protein structures requires generating both an RNA conformation and its bound complex geometry, without assuming a predefined binding site. Existing approaches generally model these coupled processes within a unified representation, entangling RNA conformation generation with protein-relative placement. In this work, we introduce RiboBridge, a structure-first framework that separates RNA conformational generation from protein--RNA assembly through cross-frame interaction geometry. RiboBridge first proposes multiple candidate RNA-binding interfaces on the protein surface, generates canonical RNA conformations within a pretrained RNA structural latent space (RiboSphere), and then predicts protein--RNA interaction constraints to bridge the independently defined RNA and protein coordinate frames. The resulting interaction geometry is converted into a geometrically consistent protein-relative pose through rigid placement. We evaluate RiboBridge on a temporally split, sequence-filtered, and redundancy-controlled benchmark of 124 protein--RNA complexes. Under post-hoc interface-correspondence evaluation, where native information is used only after generation to select among already generated predicted-pocket candidates, RiboBridge achieves the highest fNAT and DockQ and the lowest interface and ligand RMSDs among compared methods. Component ablations and stage-wise diagnostics further characterize the contributions of RNA conformational generation, interaction prediction, and rigid placement, identifying the main sources of complex-recovery error.

Learning Domain Trajectories with Flow Matching for Gradual Domain Adaptation

Yubo Huang, Yushe Cao, Zixi Wang, Jingzehua Xu, Xin Lai, Xiongyu Hu, Shuai Zhang

cs.LG

Large distribution shifts remain a primary obstacle for domain generalization and unsupervised adaptation. Although gradual domain adaptation alleviates this difficulty by introducing ordered intermediate domains, most existing approaches still operate in a discrete, step-wise manner, which can accumulate errors and often relies on unstable Min--Max optimization. We propose space. FlowGDA learns time-dependent velocity fields that induce smooth domain trajectories, using a simulation-free regression objective rather than adversarial training. To prevent semantic drift, we couple left-to-right transport between consecutive domains with a source-anchored transport and a semantic consistency loss, and we further stabilize learning via a two-stage sequential optimization scheme. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms state-of-the-art techniques, validating flow matching as an effective alternative for domain adaptation.

An Agent Architecture with Tools and Memory for Selective Correction of Wrist PPG Heart Rate Estimates

Xu Wang, Shuhan Yi, Zhen Cheng

cs.LG

Wrist photoplethysmography (PPG) heart rate estimates are vulnerable to motion artifacts, and corrections that reduce mean error can worsen individual windows. We propose an agent architecture with tools and memory around a fixed heart rate estimator. Pulse, motion, and limited historical evidence generate candidate estimates, whose predicted benefits and harms guide selection. An independent risk gate determines whether to retain, correct, or reject each output. We trained the architecture on PPG-DaLiA and tested it on Wrist Exercise, GalaxyPPG, and WildPPG with frozen weights. Across these four public datasets, mean absolute error (MAE), macro-averaged over ten estimators, decreased by up to 44.5%, with the largest reduction on GalaxyPPG. Beneficial and harmful corrections decreased and increased absolute error by more than 1 bpm, respectively. Their ten-estimator macro-averaged proportions ranged from 16.06% to 36.64% and from 1.68% to 6.77%, respectively, across the four datasets. At 60% and 80% target coverage, numerical correction on the same accepted windows contributed more to total error reduction than window selection. We implemented real-time wearable acquisition, Bluetooth reception, and smartphone deployment of a selected estimator and the framework. In backend analysis of four prototype recordings from one participant, ten-estimator macro-averaged MAE decreased from 9.09 to 4.45 bpm without additional rejection. Under the evaluated conditions, the architecture improved average accuracy while quantifying correction benefits and harms, supporting wearable heart rate analysis that considers error, correction risk, and coverage.

Solvable models of learning to pursue a moving target

John J. Vastola, Kanaka Rajan

cs.LG, Journal

Agents tasked with intercepting a moving target must learn how to reach it, ideally as efficiently as possible. As observers of animal pursuit behavior have noticed, different strategies are possible: on one extreme, the agent reactively moves toward the target's current location; on the other, the agent predicts the target's future location and moves directly there. Motivated by the desire to understand how such strategies might be learned, we introduce a continuous-time linear-quadratic model of open-loop target pursuit whose optimal control strategies interpolate between these possibilities. Usefully, a small number of interpretable parameters control which type of strategy is optimal, and it is possible to derive closed-form solutions for optimal strategies, policy learning dynamics, and value learning dynamics. Exploiting our model's linear structure, we find that the time scales of learning precisely correspond to the eigenvalues of certain matrices, and that relevant eigenvalue spectra generically have a gap. We show that this gap indicates that agents tend to learn how to reach their target before optimizing their movement. Our results provide a detailed mathematical characterization of pursuit behavior and its learning dynamics, which can both serve as a benchmark for empirical work, and as a foundation for more elaborate theoretical treatments of pursuit.

Federated Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Xiaobao Wang, Renda Han, Zhiliang Xia, Meng Liu, Renxiang Guan, Di Jin

cs.LG

Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) leverages centralized knowledge graphs to enhance large language models with structured knowledge retrieval. However, real-world knowledge is inherently distributed across multiple organizations rather than being centrally accessible. Privacy constraints further prohibit sharing entire local knowledge graphs, preventing GraphRAG from effectively retrieving and utilizing distributed knowledge. To this end, we propose Federated GraphRAG (FedGRAG), a unified framework for distributed graph retrieval and knowledge-enhanced generation over multiple clients with knowledge graphs. Specifically, we address two fundamental challenges in federated GraphRAG. First, the absence of a global knowledge graph makes unified retrieval impossible. To address this, we develop a Federated Knowledge Routing mechanism that efficiently identifies relevant clients through lightweight knowledge indices without exposing local graph structures. Second, local graph memory cannot be shared directly because it contains sensitive entities and knowledge information. We therefore propose a controlled-disclosure evidence construction method. It extracts retrieved evidence subgraphs and converts them into an evidence abstract, enabling evidence conversion and reconstruction on the server. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Federated GraphRAG consistently outperforms existing baselines in both retrieval and downstream generation tasks, establishing a new paradigm for deploying GraphRAG in federated environments.

Width and Depth Limits Commute During Training

Ruijia Zhang, Soufiane Hayou

cs.LG, stat.ML

Recent work established that the infinite-width and infinite-depth limits of residual networks commute at initialization when residual branches are scaled by $1/\sqrt{depth}$. In particular, the network output converges to the same limit whether width and depth tend to infinity sequentially or jointly. We extend this commutativity result to training in linear residual networks. Under the same $1/\sqrt{depth}$ residual-branch scaling (Depth-$\mu$P) and the $\mu$P width parameterization, we prove that, for any fixed number of gradient-descent steps with gradient clipping, the network output converges in $L^2$ along every joint width--depth growth path as both width and depth tend to infinity. Consequently, the infinite-width and infinite-depth limits commute throughout any finite training horizon. Without gradient clipping, the corresponding $L^2$ convergence does not hold in general, but we establish the weaker convergence in probability.

Random Remasking Scales Masked Diffusion Inference

Zhaoyi Zhou, Frank Wu, Andrea Zanette

cs.LG

Masked diffusion models (MDMs) generate sequences by progressively unmasking tokens, but errors introduced during decoding are typically irreversible. Recent work has shown that iterative correction can improve sample quality, yet existing theory fails to explain this given a learned, imperfect denoiser model. We re-formulate MDM inference as a Markov chain on the clean sequence space, defined by a remask-denoise kernel that randomly re-introduces masks and then denoises. This helps us analyze the actual denoising trajectory towards the data distribution. The analysis motivates Random Remask-Denoise (R$^2$D) inference, a purely test-time, finetuning-free, reward-free procedure. To further improve performance, we propose Trajectory Correction Training (TCT), a denoiser-training procedure that exposes the model to its own errors along the generation trajectory. On logical tasks, our approach exhibits a striking near-log-linear test-time scaling, achieving over 97% accuracy on hard Sudoku puzzles and over 99% accuracy on 3-SAT. Our approach also applies to pretrained models on actual language tasks, beating iterative correction baselines and the official Q-mode accuracy of LLaDA2.1-mini on HumanEval+.

Wavenumber-Aware Deep Learning for Robust Analysis of Raman Spectra

Jihwan Kim, Jaeseong Heo, Yujin Kim, Sang Bin Yoon, Chanjoong Kim, Kwang-Im Oh, Hyon Bin Na, Kyungwon Kwak, Myeongkee Park, Joonyoung F. Joung

cs.LG, physics.chem-ph, physics.optics, included

Machine-learning models for spectroscopy usually treat a spectrum as a fixed-grid intensity vector, leaving the physical coordinate implicit in the array index. Such models are tied to a single sampling grid, spectral range and resolution, which limits their use when measurement conditions change. Here we represent each spectral point as a pair of intensity and wavenumber and compute Transformer positional encodings from the wavenumber itself. On Raman spectra of three bacterial species and their synthetic mixtures, this representation remained robust to noise, sparser sampling and partial spectral coverage across convolutional, residual and Transformer networks. In controlled comparisons, wavenumber-based encoding outperformed index-based and learned encodings under partial observation and retained its performance on sampling grids never seen during training, where index-based encoding degraded. These findings were reproduced on a public 30-isolate bacterial benchmark. Explicit physical coordinates offer a simple route to robust spectral analysis under variable measurement conditions.

Visualizing and Interpreting Temporal Care Team Dynamics for Cancer Survival Risk Analysis

Yuhua Huang, Hsiao-Ying Lu, Kwan-Liu Ma

cs.LG

Cancer care is a longitudinal, time-sensitive process requiring coordination among diverse healthcare professionals (HCPs), whose collaboration evolves throughout treatment. Prior studies link care team structure to patient outcomes, but the temporal dynamics of collaboration and their relationship to evolving patient risk remain poorly understood. We introduce a visual analytics framework for modeling and interpreting time-varying care team interactions in oncology. We construct dynamic collaboration networks from electronic health record (EHR)-mediated communication data and use a temporal graph neural network (TGNN) to estimate patient outcome risk over time. We then investigate how changes in care team composition and specialist participation relate to shifts in estimated risk across treatment stages. Our multi-view interactive system combines TGNN-based risk modeling with coordinated visualizations of care team networks, feature-level explanations from a surrogate logistic regression model, and exploratory what-if analysis. Without making causal claims, the system helps analysts examine how risk estimates co-evolve with collaboration patterns and identify care team characteristics associated with these changes. This work improves the interpretability of temporal graph-based models in longitudinal, team-based healthcare, with potential applicability to other evolving collaborative settings.

Primal-Dual Representation Learning for Low-Rank Constrained MDPs

Chenhao Zhou, Chao Zhang, Wensong Bai, Hanbin Zhao, Hui Qian

cs.LG

Representation learning has substantially improved the efficiency of unconstrained continuous-state Markov Decision Processes (MDPs), but its extension to Constrained MDPs (CMDPs) is difficult because the transition representation must be learned while constraint violation is controlled. In this paper, we study representation learning for low-rank CMDPs with continuous state spaces, and propose REP-PD-TEG for the soft cumulative-violation setting. In the feature learning period, the algorithm interacts with the environment using a decaying-$\epsilon$-greedy mixture execution policy, and uses the collected samples to learn transition features by maximum likelihood. Based on the learned features, it constructs uncertainty bonuses and plans with an optimistic Lagrangian objective to obtain the return policy. We prove regret and soft-violation guarantees for the return policies, and quantify the influence of the decaying-$\epsilon$-greedy exploration. In particular, REP-PD-TEG achieves the optimal $\widetilde O(\sqrt K)$ return policy bound, and gives a $\widetilde O(K^{3/4})$ overall sample bound when exploratory actions are charged. For the stricter hard cumulative violation, where feasibility must be verified episode by episode, we propose REP-PD-H-TEG. It plans under a bonus-corrected lower-confidence utility certificate with the learned representation, and performs a stabilized dual search to obtain the return policy, yielding regret and hard-violation bounds. Together, these results give the first theoretically guaranteed representation-learning method for low-rank continuous-state CMDPs with both soft cumulative guarantees and certified hard-violation control.

Adaptive Deadline- and Energy-Aware Caching Algorithms in Dynamic Multi-UAV Edge Networks

Jian Kim, Ki-Il Kim

cs.LG, cs.NI

Content caching via unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) becomes one of the solutions to maintain content delivery when terrestrial infrastructure is unavailable or disrupted. However, existing caching approaches often focus primarily on cache-hit performance or average delivery delay, without jointly addressing spatially heterogeneous and time-varying demand, content-specific deadlines, and uneven communication-energy consumption across UAVs. To address these challenges, we formulate a dynamic deadline- and energy-aware cache placement and replacement problem that jointly considers deadline misses, UAV-side communication energy, and inter-UAV energy imbalance. To achieve this, we first develop deadline- and energy-aware UBAC (DE-UBAC), an interpretable hierarchical rule-based realization that incorporates deadline-aware prioritization, cooperative placement, energy-aware coordination, and cache-update cost. However, predefined priorities and thresholds limit its adaptability under dynamically coupled network conditions. We therefore propose GRU-assisted Deadline- and Energy-aware Proximal Policy Optimization (GRU-DEPPO), which combines local and global GRU-based temporal representations with a centralized PPO actor-critic for adaptive cache placement and replacement. Simulation results show that GRU-DEPPO achieves the lowest deadline-miss rate and total UAV-side communication energy among the considered schemes. Compared with DE-UBAC, GRU-DEPPO reduces the deadline-miss rate by approximately 61% and inter-UAV energy imbalance by approximately 40%.

One Pretrained Decision Model Is Enough for Training-Free Generalist Graph Anomaly Detection

Xinyu Zhao, Qingyun Sun, Ziwei Zhang, Tianchen Zhu, Daqing Li, Jianxin Li

cs.LG

Graph anomaly detection (GAD) aims to identify irregular entities in graph-structured data, yet heterogeneous anomaly patterns and distributional differences across graphs challenge generalizable detection. Existing training-free approaches avoid task-specific optimization but primarily rely on predefined numerical scoring rules, leaving the semantic interpretation of heterogeneous anomaly evidence largely unexplored. In this paper, we propose Jev-GAD, a training-free generalist graph anomaly detection framework that bridges heterogeneous numerical evidence and pretrained decision mechanisms. To address heterogeneous anomaly patterns, we construct complementary structural, attribute, and neighborhood evidence and transform heterogeneous statistics into graph-relative anomaly percentiles. To move beyond predefined numerical scoring rules, we leverage a single frozen pretrained language model for evidence-conditioned anomaly assessment. To bridge the semantic gap between numerical evidence and pretrained models, we design a semantic Graph-to-State (G2S) transformation that organizes graph context and semantically labeled node evidence into structured decision states for non-generative option-logit readout. The resulting decision scores are integrated with four complementary statistical scores through equal-weight rank-based fusion, without GAD-specific gradient-based optimization. Extensive experiments on seven graph anomaly detection benchmarks spanning citation, social, and co-review networks demonstrate the effectiveness of Jev-GAD, achieving average AUROC and AUPRC scores of 89.18\% and 52.40\%, respectively. Further analyses validate the contributions of pretrained decision readout and semantic evidence grounding.

Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! Increasing Flash Storage Lifetime for KV Cache Offloading in LLM Serving

Maharani A. P. Irawan, Kuntai Du, Jiayi Yao, Dongjoo Seo, Yuyang Huang, Haryadi S. Gunawi, Junchen Jiang

cs.LG

KV caching reduces the serving cost of large language models (LLMs), but the growing KV cache footprint is increasingly exceeds GPU and host-memory capacity. Therefore, people use SSD to expand the KV cache storage capacity with high I/O bandwidth. However, in production LLM workload, such as multi-round conversation and LLM agents, the total amount of KV cache writes significantly exceeds SSD capacity, shortening SSD lifetime from years to months. To improve SSD lifetime, a natural solution is to reduce the KV cache writes by gating the KV cache writes via historical access patterns. However, that significantly lowers the KV cache hit rate (and increase the LLM serving cost) since KV caches from different attention types might have identical historical access pattern but different future access patterns. Our insight is that, to reduce KV cache writes without hurting KV cache hit rate, one need to not only consider the access pattern, but also application-level information such as the attention type of KV cache. Leveraging this observation, we propose Gandalf , the first SSD KV cache offloading system with SSD life time awareness. Gandalf prioritize the admission of full-attention KV cache into SSD over the cache generated by other types of attention, which significantly increases SSD lifetime without sacrificing cache hit rate. Our evaluation shows that Gandalf extends the SSD lifetime by 7.3X on average, while having negligible impact on cache hit rate across multiple workload traces and wide range of hardware capacity. Gandalf design can be extended to other flash-based storage, such as High Bandwidth Flash (HBF).

A Computationally Efficient and Rate-Optimal Algorithm for Adversarial Linear MDPs with (Aggregate) Bandit Feedback

Haolin Liu, Chen-Yu Wei, Hanbin Zhou

cs.LG

We study episodic linear Markov decision processes with unknown transitions, adversarial losses, and bandit feedback. The state-of-the-art result for this setting (Liu et al., 2024) either achieves $\sqrt{K}$ regret with a computationally inefficient algorithm or achieves suboptimal $K^{3/4}$ regret, where $K$ is the number of episodes. Our work gives the first $\sqrt{K}$ regret with a polynomial-time algorithm. Our method reduces adversarial linear MDPs to adversarial linear bandits whose action set consists of policies' expected feature vectors. The central challenge is that this action set is unknown and can be estimated only through exploration. We address this challenge by constructing an approximate separation oracle for the action set that either generates executable policies from queried features, or refines an outer approximation of the action set. This approach draws inspiration from the reduction of van Erven et al. (2025) for linear contextual bandits and the oracle-efficient linear bandit algorithm of Ito et al. (2019), but additionally deals with the imprecision of the oracle. The resulting algorithm requires only aggregate loss feedback, and to our knowledge. This is the first polynomial-time algorithm for adversarial MDPs with aggregate feedback beyond the tabular case.

Is spectral predictability enough? Testing {\Omega}-matching for synthetic pretraining under distribution shift

Edric Castel C. Hao, Juan Raphael S. Se\~na, Neon S. De Jesus

cs.LG

Synthetic pretraining under distribution shift requires deciding what synthetic data to use. A natural criterion is to match the spectral predictability $\Omega$ of the source to the target, but is $\Omega$ sufficient? We test this hypothesis, which we call predictability-aware pretraining, across three architectures (LSTM, PatchTST, TCN) and three seeds. Under ideal synthetic-to-synthetic conditions, $\Omega$-matched pretraining can outperform even an oracle baseline (+46.8% MSE reduction, LSTM), though the benefit is already architecture-dependent. On the real-world ETTm1 benchmark, however, this relationship does not hold: sine-plus-noise pretraining at matched $\Omega$ yields only a marginal mean improvement +2.3% averaged over all three architectures, up to +65.5% on individual channels), whereas $\Omega$ matched chaotic ODE trajectories systematically degrade performance -13.2%). The two sources agree on the direction of transfer for only 53% of configurations across all three architectures (Pearson r=-0.02), indicating that $\Omega$ carries little information about which source will transfer beneficially. We further observe an architecture-dependent asymmetry in the response to the direction of the $\Omega$ gap. We conclude that $\Omega$ matching can help in controlled settings but is not sufficient to predict transfer across sources, and we discuss additional signal properties that a source-selection criterion may need to consider.

Neural Spectral Capacity with Residual Context for Heterogeneous Resource Allocation within Transformers

Chenyu Zhu, Ruoyu Zhao, Zhichao Lu

cs.LG, cs.AI

Predicting relative trained performance from architectural specifications can guide design when training costs limit experimentation. Neural Spectral Capacity (NSC) provides such a heuristic, but scores projections independently and assigns identical scores to compatible reorderings of the same layer configurations. We introduce Residual-Aware Neural Spectral Capacity (ResNSC) for Transformers, using a specification-derived statistical reference for accumulated residual updates to determine the input and residual-state statistics in each projection's capacity calculation, while retaining additive evaluation without data or instantiated weights. When residual widths vary, earlier choices affect later scores, so we augment NSC's allocator (NSC-DP) with residual history beyond its layer-and-budget state. ResNSC-DP jointly allocates residual and feed-forward widths under a resource budget, with certified bounds on the best numerically evaluated ResNSC score within a specified finite candidate set. At the 121M and 374M pretraining scales, selected architectures achieve lower mean perplexity than uniform, variable-width, and tapered allocations on FineWeb-Edu, WikiText-2, and LAMBADA at comparable parameter budgets. In inherited-weight LLaMA-7B pruning using the LoNAS supernet, ResNSC-DP improves eight-task average accuracy over NSC by 3.28--5.46 percentage points across eight matched parameter budgets. Paired reversals preserve the projection shapes and NSC scores yet worsen performance in both settings, exposing performance differences hidden by position-independent scoring. The code for this work is available at https://github.com/Optima-CityU/neural-spectral-capacity.

Lean Finder V2: Semantic Search for Mathlib That Disambiguates User Queries

Jialin Lu, Xiaoyu Liu, Hengrui Luo, Rodrigo Stehling, Linxi Liu, Weiran Sun, Yuepeng Wang, Wuyang Chen

cs.LG, cs.IR

We introduce Lean Finder V2, a semantic search framework for Mathlib that disambiguates user queries through complementary model-level adversarial training and input-level query rewriting. Human mathematicians and coding agents rarely search Mathlib by providing detailed context. In practice, they query short properties, notation, aliases, or hypotheses, leaving much of the intended mathematics implicit. In contrast, existing Mathlib search engines unrealistically assume context-rich queries. The scarcity of real-world short queries paired with ground-truth Mathlib statements makes this ambiguity even more intractable. We argue that this is essentially a data problem. Through adversarial training, we scale up the generation of labeled short and ambiguous queries, and learn to disambiguate user queries at both model-level fine-tuning and input-level query rewriting. On the human-curated MathlibQR benchmark, our Lean Finder V2 sets a new state of the art, with at least 11% relative improvement over the previous best Mathlib semantic search engines. On top of our state-of-the-art retriever, optimized query rewriting further improves MathlibQR retrieval. On unlabeled queries from human and coding-agent interactions, Lean Finder V2 is ranked as the strongest system. Integrated with Claude Code, Lean Finder V2 improves proof success on downstream theorem-proving task. Lean Finder V2 is available at: https://leanfinder.github.io/

Reverse-KL Policy Distillation: Statistical Guarantees and Limits

Haolin Liu, Chen-Yu Wei, Julian Zimmert

cs.LG

Reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) distillation has shown empirical promise for training language models, but its statistical guarantees and limits remain poorly understood. In particular, the effects of two design choices remain unclear: Does access to the teacher's full action distribution provide an advantage over observing only the probability it assigns to the sampled action? Must training trajectories come from the student (online distillation), or can teacher-generated trajectories (offline distillation) suffice? Our work takes a first step toward understanding the theory underlying these questions. We study the sample complexity of online reverse-KL distillation under two feedback models: the teacher reveals either its full action distribution or only the probability of the student-generated action. We establish minimax bounds for the former and show that substantially more data may be required for the latter. In contrast, offline reverse-KL distillation can attain the same minimax rate whether the teacher reveals its full distribution or only its probability of the teacher-generated action. The key distinction is that teacher-generated actions themselves reveal the teacher's preferences, while queries at student-selected actions can leave those preferences unobserved. This highlights an additional statistical challenge for online distillation compared to offline distillation. These statistical guarantees do not follow simply from optimizing standard distillation objectives: our lower bounds show that common online objectives can fail when candidates are evaluated only on past student-generated trajectories. We develop trajectory-loss and importance-ratio truncation techniques together with policy-space learning to achieve optimal online distillation guarantees.

Bifurcations from Weight Space: JEPAWG on Parametric Dynamical Systems

Tobias G\"obel, Miranda C.N. Cheng

cs.LG, cs.AI, nlin.CD, at, the, NeurIPS, 2026, Workshop, on, Neural, Network, Artifacts, as, a, New, Data, Modality

Trained network weights are increasingly treated as data, but there is rarely ground truth about what a population of models should encode, which hampers systematic validation of weight-space methods. This makes parametric dynamical systems a natural test-bed: the dynamics change qualitatively at submanifolds of parameter space that are, in well-studied families, known analytically or numerically. Fine-tuning a forecasting network at each point of a parameter grid yields a model zoo indexed by a physical parameter, against which weight-space diagnostics can be checked. We build such model zoos for four dynamical systems and analyse them with JEPAWG, a recently proposed joint-embedding hypernetwork built around a learned parameter--weight representation. It was first introduced for neural samplers for lattice field theories, where its latent geometry located the phase transition and estimated the 2D Ising critical exponent. Applied to dynamical systems, JEPAWG is competitive on forecasting with a hypernetwork trained directly for that task, despite being supervised on the parameter--weight correspondence rather than on forecasts. Beyond forecasting, the norm of the parameter-derivative of the decoded weights locates Hopf points and curves, tracks a period-doubling cascade closely enough to give a qualitative estimate of the Feigenbaum constant, and detects a global bifurcation, which has no eigenvalue signature and is therefore invisible to linearisation. As such, JEPAWG provides interpretable insight into the dynamics that is not available from other hypernetworks. Together, these results indicate that JEPAWG is a workable instrument for reading physical structure off a population of trained networks.

Training Mixed-Monotone Neural Networks for Verification Without Set Propagation

Jonas Fingerhut, Lukas Koller, Matthias Althoff

cs.LG

Most formal verification approaches for neural networks conservatively enclose the output set of the neural network. However, computing a tight output enclosure is in general computationally hard. We sidestep this challenge with a novel neural network architecture that realizes mixed-monotone functions as the difference of monotone subnetworks. By utilizing the theory on mixed-monotone systems, this reduces the computation of a tight output enclosure from complicated set propagation to two simple forward passes of opposite corners of an input interval. Moreover, because the size of the resulting enclosure is a closed-form function of the weights of the neural network, we can directly minimize it during training. We prove a lower bound on the size of any such enclosure, given by the total variation of the represented function generalized to several inputs, which makes it possible to compare the tightness of the enclosure against the optimum. Our evaluation on regression and dynamical-system benchmarks demonstrates that our training reduces the enclosure size to within 0.5% of the lower bound without sacrificing prediction accuracy. Computing our enclosure is orders of magnitude faster than CROWN and $\alpha$-CROWN and on deep networks it is also tighter than zonotope propagation and CROWN, while its size exceeds that of $\alpha$-CROWN by only 5.1%.

Joules-to-GDP: How Much Economic Value Do AI Systems Produce per Unit of Energy?

Amirreza Zeinali, Jon Saad-Falcon, Avanika Narayan, Mersad Abbasi, Jonathan S. Hartley, John Hennessy, Christopher Re, Azalia Mirhoseini

cs.LG, econ.GN, q-fin.EC

An AI system can produce more economically valuable work while delivering less value for the energy it consumes. We therefore ask: How much economic value does an AI system create per unit of energy consumed? We formalize economic value in two ways: (1) net dollar value, the output's dollar value minus AI-system execution cost, and (2) time saving, the expert's completion time minus the AI system's completion time. Dividing each by measured end-to-end energy yields Net Value per Joule (NVJ) and Time Saving per Joule (TSJ), reported in USD/MJ and hours/MJ. We study 220 public GDPval tasks across 44 occupations, serving five open-weight models locally and evaluating four accelerator types. First, the occupation groups where AI systems succeed most often, deliver the greatest dollar value, or save the most time do not necessarily provide the greatest economic return per joule. For example, among the tested tasks, engineering work delivers about 10% more net dollar value per task than software development. Yet software development delivers about 27% more net value per joule. Second, GLM 5.2 delivers the highest average net dollar value, at $72.27 per task using 0.430 MJ, equivalent in energy to about four hours of active laptop use. By contrast, Gemma 4 31B delivers 56% of that net dollar value using just 23% of the energy, achieving approximately 2.5 times the NVJ and 2.8 times the TSJ. Third, across four single-accelerator deployments, Qwen3.6-27B's NVJ varies by a factor of 4.32 and TSJ by 9.27, with B200 leading both. Additionally, one and eight H100s deliver similar net dollar value, but eight reduce completion time by 22% at 3.24 times the energy. Our framework makes output value and expert time saving under an energy budget explicit objectives for improving models, accelerators, and software, while offering a new basis for assessing AI's potential economic effect on occupational work.

MOER: Mix-Policy Optimization under Expert Prefix Guidance with Selective Rollback

Xuyang Zhong, Jiuding Yang, Yiwen Guo, Chen Liu

cs.LG, cs.AI

Mix-policy optimization incorporates off-policy expert data into the reinforcement learning (RL) loop to transcend the capability boundary of on-policy RL. However, existing methods either provide static full-trace guidance that suppresses self-exploration, or adopt expert prefix guidance without correctness guarantees, leading to suboptimal performance or even training collapse. In this paper, we propose MOER, a mix-policy optimization framework that harmonizes expert guidance with self-exploration. MOER injects a decaying expert chain-of-thought prefix into the prompt and lets the model complete the remaining reasoning, with the prefix length progressively annealed to encourage self-exploration. A selective rollback mechanism replaces failed completions with the original full expert trace, guaranteeing the correctness of every off-policy trajectory. Furthermore, a harmonized mix-policy objective optimizes on-policy tokens with a GRPO loss while supervising both expert prefix and completion tokens with a trust-region SFT loss, substantially stabilizing training. Extensive experiments show that MOER achieves the highest average scores in mathematical and general domain reasoning tasks, outperforming all basic and mix-policy baselines, and generalizes consistently across diverse tasks, model scales, and base model categories. Codes are available at https://github.com/CityU-MLO/MOER.

One Teacher, Many Views: Reducing Reference Sensitivity in On-Policy Self-Distillation

Khouloud Saadi, Di Wang

cs.LG, cs.CL

On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) improves reasoning by training a model on its own trajectories with dense token-level supervision from a privileged copy conditioned on a reference solution. Existing methods typically use a single teacher distribution conditioned on the complete reference. We identify a limitation of this formulation: a reference is only one realization of the reasoning process. Although it provides evidence about the answer, it also encodes an ordering, phrasing, and solution path. Full-reference conditioning can therefore produce correct targets that depend excessively on information unavailable to the student at inference. We show that replacing one valid reference with another, while holding the problem and student state fixed, can substantially alter the teacher distribution, a phenomenon we call reference-induced target instability. To address this instability, we introduce Monte Carlo Privileged Distillation (MCPD), which models privileged context as a distribution over partial reference views and distills the marginal teacher distribution. MCPD evaluates the teacher under multiple views and averages the resulting next-token distributions. We show that this probability-space average is the exact target induced by minimizing the expected teacher-to-student KL divergence over privileged views. Across mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks, we show that MCPD produces more reference-consistent supervision and consistently improves over full-reference OPSD.

Inherited, Not Invented: Tracing the Provenance of Wait in Reasoning Models

Zhizhuo Kou, Sizai Hou, Long Chen, Jian Yang, Junyu Luo, Jingshu Peng, Wenkai Zhang, Sirui Han, Yike Guo

cs.LG, cs.CL

Reinforcement learning (RL) is credited with teaching reasoning models to write {wait} before reconsidering a step, yet large RL accuracy gains do not require the word. This view can confuse what RL discovers with what a model inherits from its training data. We trace the word through open models' training histories, reading it with label-free indices of frequency, position, form and usage and an injected-error test, all computed from sampled outputs. We find that zero-RL almost never coins the marker: on bases that lack the word, RL raises MATH-500 accuracy substantially while {wait} stays near absent. It arrives with training data: mid-training in OLMo-3, and fine-tuning on reasoning traces installs it in most models whose base lacks it. Where the base already uses it, both released RL-Zero recipes (OLMo-3, MiMo) multiply it, whereas KL-free GRPO from the same base does not; OLMo-3's recipe wears down its form and makes it a more sensitive but more liberal alarm; RL after supervised fine-tuning barely changes it, and our GRPO recipe erases a marker seeded by fine-tuning. Finally, we show that marker frequency alone does not indicate RL-induced reasoning and caution against LLM-as-a-judge revision metrics, as automated judges can reverse human-labelled trends.

Tracing Subliminal Learning Through Pretraining

Angela Tang, Vishesh Anand, Gon\c{c}alo Paulo

cs.LG

Large language models trained on data generated by other models can sometimes inherit traits unrelated to that data, a phenomenon called subliminal learning. We study both hard label distillation, where students train on numbers generated by a biased teacher, and on-policy soft label distillation, where students generate the data and the teacher's logits provide the learning signal. Using Pythia, PolyPythia, and DataDecide OLMo models, we find that transfer persists in both settings across differences in weight initialization, pretraining data order, pretraining corpus, model size, and architecture. Across pretraining checkpoints, the ability to transmit a preference and the ability to acquire one develop separately: some checkpoints are more effective teachers but weaker students, and vice versa. Consistent with this distinction, cross-model transfer can be strongly asymmetric in magnitude when teacher and student roles are reversed. To investigate what carries the preference signal, we construct synthetic number sequences that match the teacher's generated number frequencies but have no dependence on the prompt or preceding numbers and find that these still induce substantial transfer in the hard-label setting. In top-$K$ soft-label distillation, removing animal tokens from the loss reduces transfer, although not completely in the same-model setting. In our experiments, soft label distillation produces more consistent transfer with less extensive training than hard label distillation. The growing use of on-policy distillation in model post-training makes this a particularly relevant setting for assessing unintended preference transfer.

Building Presentation Intelligence for High-Quality Slide Generation

Yuhang Xie, Cheng Li, Chenliang Li, Mingyu Xu, Mingwei Sun, Suhang Zheng, Ming Yan, Xiaoxiao Xu, Lin Qu

cs.LG

PowerPoint presentations (PPTs) are a dominant medium for communication across diverse domains, yet high-quality PPT generation still depends heavily on labor-intensive human effort. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) for agentic tasks have created both new opportunities and challenges (Chen et al., 2026) for automatic PPT generation. In this work, we present a unified training framework for automatic PPT generation toward presentation intelligence, integrating cross-validated data synthesis, guided teacher enhancement, and verifiable reward optimization. We first construct a cross-validated pipeline for data synthesis, with explicit control over both difficulty and validity. We then develop a teacher-enhancement framework grounded in rules and prior knowledge to substantially strengthen the instructional capacity of teacher models. Building on this, we further improve supervised fine-tuning (SFT) through a mixed-teacher strategy and a lightweight outcome-driven trajectory filtering method. Finally, based on the resulting SFT models, we introduce a suite of verifiable aesthetic metrics together with a GRPO-based reinforcement learning framework to optimize slide-level visual attributes beyond text-only supervision. Experiments show that our enhanced SFT pipeline improves Qwen-3.6-27B and Qwen-3.6-35B-A3B by 7.45 and 9.35 points on PresentBench, respectively, surpassing GLM-5.2 while maintaining strong generalization. Reinforcement learning further improves performance across five aesthetic dimensions, compensating for the limitations of SFT in visual refinement. Overall, the results demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework for high-quality PPT generation.

Same molecule, Different Knowledge: Cross-Identifier Binding Failures in LLMs For chemistry

Khouloud Saadi, Di Wang

cs.LG

Chemical AI workflows routinely represent the same molecule through multiple co-referring identifiers, including trivial names, IUPAC names, and SMILES, implicitly assuming that these representations provide interchangeable access to an LLM's knowledge. To test this assumption, we first introduce a controlled benchmark and show that it does not hold. Across foundation models spanning 7B to 72B parameters, even chemistry-specialized models, co-referring molecular identifiers consistently expose different subsets of the model's knowledge. We then conduct a series of controlled behavioral, representational, and causal analyses to characterize this failure. We show that even when a model can identify a molecule from its SMILES and retrieve a fact through the corresponding name, the same fact can remain inaccessible directly from SMILES. We term this phenomenon \emph{cross-identifier binding failure}. Further analysis of internal representations, conflicting identifiers, and parameter editing shows that neither improved chemical representation competence nor modification of individual facts is sufficient to establish reliable access across identifiers. Together, these results indicate that the core failure lies in the binding between an identifier and knowledge already present in the model. Motivated by this diagnosis, we introduce Binding Self-Distillation, a simple mitigation that uses lexical identity during training to transfer the model's existing knowledge access to the structural route. This enables factual retrieval directly from SMILES at inference time, without requiring a name lookup or training the underlying model from scratch.

Random Initialization as a Baseline for Time Series Foundation Models

Pinar Sungu Isiacik, Georgiana Ifrim

cs.LG

Time series foundation models (TSFMs) use self-supervised pretraining to learn reusable representations for downstream tasks such as multivariate time series classification (MTSC). We examine how much pretrained weights contribute beyond the architecture itself by comparing seven publicly released instances from four TSFM families with architecture-matched randomly initialized counterparts on the UEA MTSC archive. For each instance, five random initializations are evaluated using four classifiers on frozen representations: ridge classification, logistic regression, RBF SVM, and random forest. After model-specific exclusions due to computational constraints, the study comprises 183 paired dataset-level comparisons. Pretraining improves mean accuracy for all seven instances, but the gains are modest, ranging from 1.43 to 5.19 percentage points, with an overall average improvement of 2.58 points. Three of seven per-instance Wilcoxon signed-rank tests have raw $p$-values below 0.05, but only NuTime remains significant after Holm correction. A secondary pooled test across all 183 pairs gives $p < 0.001$, while the corrected per-instance tests remain the primary evidence. We also compare with ROCKET, Hydra, and Quant, which remain competitive without requiring large-scale pretraining. These findings suggest that MTSC evaluations should distinguish the effect of pretrained weights from that of the underlying architecture and should include both architecture-matched random initialization and strong non-pretrained baselines.

OpenAttention: One Percent Is Enough with Tail Proxies

Hyunwoo Yu, Jongbeom Lee, Hyunbin Kim, Sungjun Park, Jaemin Choi, Jincheol Yang, Seunghun Moon, Bongjoon Hyun, Kyomin Sohn, Kyeongbo Kong, Suk-Ju Kang

cs.LG, cs.AI, Code:, https://github.com/hyunwoo137/OpenAttention

Sparse attention reads only a small subset of the key-value cache, and how to treat the unread tail remains a key challenge. Discarding the tail biases the output, and sampling it as previous approaches do fails at reads of 5% and below, where the tail likely holds heavy hitters the selector missed. Our key observation is that the simplest possible proxy, one token that folds the entire tail into its count-aware mean, already yields lower output error than a sample at every read behind a practical selector. We further show in closed form that at low reads sampling nearly doubles the weight error of discarding while a single proxy stays below it. Building on these findings, we explore approximating the dense attention distribution with a few proxies that target the tail. We propose OpenAttention, a pipeline of clustering, ErrorGate and top-k selection followed by a count-aware mean proxy. To implement clustering and selection both efficiently and effectively, we design training-free Query-Geometry Clustering (QGC) and a Query-Geometry Selector (QGS), which compress the keys while respecting the query directions. Additionally, unlike prior methods that rank clusters by centroid alone, ErrorGate allocates the exact read by the estimated weight error of each cluster. On RULER-32K HARD with Llama-3.1-8B, OpenAttention retains 99% of dense accuracy at a 1% read. It also achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across models, context lengths and datasets. Our customized attention kernel, OpenAttention.flash, runs the decode attention stage 10$\times$ faster than dense attention at a 1% read.

Ground-Truth-First Benchmarking of Reinforcement Learning via Converse Optimality: From Smooth, Fully Observed Systems to Non-Smooth or Partially Observed Tasks

Gr\'egoire Ouerdane, Sinan Ibrahim, Stefan Streif, Pavel Osinenko

cs.LG

Benchmarking in reinforcement learning is usually relative: algorithms are ranked by returns on environments with unknown optima. Distance to optimality remains unobservable, while conclusions may depend on reward design, stochasticity, hyperparameters, environment selection, and simulator details. We develop a ground-truth-first framework based on converse optimality, constructing each task with a certified optimal value function and policy withheld from learners and used only for evaluation. The framework unifies two modes: sensitivity benchmarking samples certified environments from continuous-time generators to assess robustness to task variation, while oracle-referenced comparison uses native discrete-time generators to evaluate policies via optimality gap, regret, and policy error. Continuous-time models support physically interpretable sensitivity studies but introduce dependence on the simulator step ($\Delta t$) and numerical scheme. Native discrete-time models align the environment, oracle, and learner on the same transition law, eliminating external integration choices as a confounder. We reproduce quantitative results covering optimizer sensitivity and oracle-referenced comparisons on serial planar arms, dynamically extended nonholonomic vehicles, and quadrotor-inspired tasks. The methodology extends beyond smooth affine Gaussian systems: virtual-input pullback handles non-affine raw-action interfaces and finite-covariance non-Gaussian disturbances. A non-smooth converse optimality formulation enables certified benchmarks with kinked value functions and switching surfaces. For partially observed evaluation, an information-state formulation reduces benchmarking to converse optimality on a sufficient statistic and provides an information-penalty reference. Code, fixtures, and diagnostics are available on the public project page.

Doubly Robust Distributionally Robust Offline Contextual Pricing

Min Xu, Xinyi Yin, Yunfan Zhang, Yuxuan Han, Houcai Shen, Caihua Chen

cs.LG, Learning,, 2026

Offline contextual pricing often relies on logged observational data, but faces challenges from distributional shifts between training and deployment environments. Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) provides a principled approach to off-policy evaluation and learning (OPE/L). However, existing methods are mostly limited to discrete actions. Recent work has explored DRO for continuous treatments using inverse propensity weighting (IPW), while such IPW-based estimators can be sensitive to the convergence rate of propensity score estimates, particularly when estimated nonparametrically, which may lead to larger estimation errors and regret. In this work, we develop a doubly robust (DR) framework for distributionally robust OPE/L in continuous pricing settings. For evaluation, we propose a localized DR estimator that addresses the computational challenges of worst-case expectations by fitting only a small number of regressions, comparable to standard non-robust DR, while achieving semiparametric efficiency under mild product rate conditions. For learning, we leverage the inherent smoothness of demand noise to handle pricing-specific discontinuities in revenue outcomes (e.g., threshold-based purchase decisions), establishing a finite-sample regret bound of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}_p(T^{-s/(2s+1)})$ for smoothness orders $s=1,2$. This bound improves upon existing regret rates in existing DRO-based off-policy learning (OPL) for continuous treatments. Extensive experiments under various levels of distribution shift validate our proposed framework.

Energy and Latency First: How Deployment Constraints Reverse Time-Series Model Selection

Shruti Bothe, Illyyne Saffar, Ayush K. Varshney

cs.LG, 2026), Workshop:, Generalization, for, Time, Series, in, Tight, Settings:, Latency,, Inference,, Memory,, Privacy,, and, Sustainability, (TS-LIMITS)

AI models, and time-series models in particular are typically ranked by accuracy and deployed under constraints absent from that ranking. In this paper, we study a real setting where these constraints are explicit and measurable: handover pre- diction in cellular networks, where inference runs on the User Equipment once per 40 ms measurement period and must complete before the network acts.
