OP-Print: Tracing Membership Imprints in On-Policy Self-Distillation

Qi Li, Xinchao Wang

cs.LG, cs.CR

On-policy distillation has been widely adopted for alleviating the distribution mismatch of off-policy distillation. Self-distillation further removes the need for an external teacher by using the student's own outputs, enhanced with extra context or demonstrations, as supervision. This teacher-free design makes on-policy self-distillation practical, yet its privacy risks remain largely unexplored. In particular, using two coupled views of the same training example may strengthen its memorization. Motivated by this, we present the first study of membership inference attacks against on-policy self-distillation. We investigate both gray-box and black-box threat models and propose a unified attack framework, named OP-Print. OP-Print builds on our finding that the supervision imprint in on-policy self-distillation is sparse and structured, concentrating on uncertainty-sensitive correction sites rather than across the entire rollout. Exploiting this property, we construct attack signals tailored to the two access levels: in the gray-box setting, OP-Print directly aggregates the privileged teacher-student residual correction along the same rollout; in the black-box setting, it measures restart-based output-basin stability as an observable surrogate for the hidden correction process. Across diverse models and distillation schemes, all baseline attacks remain essentially at random guessing even under gray-box access, whereas OP-Print is broadly validated to be effective in both gray-box and black-box settings.

Questioning Instrumental Convergence in RLVR: Checkpoint Evidence from OLMo-3

Bar{\i}\c{s} Alparslan, Sandy Tanwisuth, Caleb Petterson

cs.LG, cs.AI

Instrumental convergence is often invoked as a general reason to expect capable agents to acquire or express power-seeking subgoals: self-preservation, resource acquisition, deception, or resistance to oversight which poses an important threat to the society. For LLMs, reinforcement learning is the first stage of post-training that directly optimizes behavior against an objective; therefore, it is the stage where convergent instrumental behavior, on IC grounds, likely to appear. Does reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) make a model more likely to express instrumental-convergence behavior, or does this depend on the training regime, evaluation frame, and stage of post-training? We study this question as a checkpoint-conditioned behavioral trajectory rather than as a single before/after safety score. Using InstrumentalEval, we evaluate OLMo-3-7B-Think across SFT, DPO, 13 intermediate RLVR checkpoints, and the final RLVR model. The trajectory is nonmonotonic: instrumental rate rises from 25.0% at SFT to 46.1% near RLVR step 200, then falls to 11.8% at the final checkpoint. Paired item-level tests and step-wise regression support the final SFT-to-RLVR reduction. Prompt framing does not explain this pattern: a harmful-prompt indicator combined with other framing effects explains only 15% of within-checkpoint score variance on average, and Iterative Null-space Projection (INLP) removes a detectable framing direction without changing the SFT-to-final-RLVR gap. Together with preliminary 32B endpoint results (35.53% SFT to 22.37% RLVR final), these findings indicate that RLVR does not monotonically increase instrumental behavior in this setting: the rate rises early, then falls below the SFT baseline. The result is specific to this model family and benchmark, and judge agreement is moderate, so absolute rates should be read cautiously.

BeNice! Closing Looping's Inference-Memory Gap via KV-Sharing

Hayden Prairie, Jacob M. Ryan, Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick, and Daniel Y. Fu

cs.LG

Looped architectures scale quality while keeping parameter count fixed by repeatedly passing activations through the same block of layers. During inference, each recurrence generates independent KV-caches, causing memory to grow linearly. To address this challenge, prior work has proposed KV-sharing (i.e., sharing KV-caches across depth), finding that, in some cases, the KV-cache of the final recurrence can be shared with the earlier recurrences without quality loss, a property we denote as KV-shareability. However, the mechanisms underlying KV-shareability remain poorly understood, with even identically trained looped architectures not consistently exhibiting it. In this paper, we study how KV-shareability can be enabled in looped models. We propose a two-stage method: in the first stage, we use stochastic depth during pretraining, which induces contractive residual dynamics that drive representations toward a common limit across recurrences. In the second stage, we propose BeNice!, a post-training method that broadcasts the final recurrence's KV-cache into the first recurrence, training it to produce compatible residuals while preserving the original model's quality. As a result, BeNice! enables KV-shareability while matching standard finetuning's unshared validation perplexity. Using BeNice!, we show that KV-sharing is complementary to alternative KV-compression methods, composing with memory-efficient sequence mixers (e.g., multi-latent attention, Kimi delta attention) and low-precision KV-cache quantization (e.g., fp8, mxfp8, nvfp4). Finally, in simulated low-memory, long-context inference, KV-sharing improves cache hit rates by up to 31.4%, increases throughput by up to 23.6%, and reduces p90 time-to-first-token by up to 83.6%.

Causal Chain of Thought: Mitigating Attention Leakage via Context Isolation

Gabriele Dominici, Teague McMillan, Fazl Barez, Marc Langheinrich, Martin Gjoreski

cs.LG, cs.AI

Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits step-by-step rationales that appear to explain model predictions, but there is no guarantee these steps causally drive the final answer. In standard autoregressive generation, dense attention allows each reasoning step to attend to the full context, enabling the model to derive answers through direct lookup while generating plausible-sounding but post-hoc justifications. We introduce CausalCoT, an inference framework that enforces causal transparency by decomposing problems into a directed acyclic graph of atomic sub-questions, evaluating each node in an isolated context containing only its direct causal parents. This Markovian structure removes attention pathways to non-parent content, making downstream predictions sensitive to local reasoning rather than global context leakage. Across a diverse suite of reasoning benchmarks, CausalCoT preserves competitive predictive performance while improving explanation faithfulness, increasing causal sensitivity of intermediate reasoning steps by an average of 12 and up to 21 percentage points over the strongest baseline. The structured dependency graph additionally enables test-time interventions in which local corrections propagate predictably through downstream reasoning.

A Fix for Softmax and Cross-Entropy

Jonathan T. Barron, Dan Gnanapragasam, Michael Tschannen

cs.LG

Standard implementations of softmax, log-softmax, and cross-entropy have two numerical bugs. The first bug, Softmax Collapse, rounds the target logit's gradient to zero if that one logit is much larger than the others, resulting in gradients that no longer sum to zero. The second bug, Softmax Cancellation, occurs when student logits are close to teacher logits, and produces non-zero gradients even when the student and teacher logits are equal. We present simple implementations of log-softmax, softmax, and cross-entropy that fix both bugs. Our fix eliminates the previously-observed "Slingshot" phenomenon, speeds up grokking with weight decay, prevents accuracy collapse without weight decay, stops student networks from drifting away from the teacher during distillation, and shows that after memorization, logit growth is bounded by an equilibrium between weight decay and Adam's $\epsilon$ hyperparameter.

METTA: Memory-Enhanced Test-Time Adaptation for Meta-Reinforcement Learning

Ali Najar, Hosein Hasani, Amir Kooshan Fattah, Yasamin Rajabi, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah

cs.LG, cs.AI

Meta-reinforcement learning aims to train agents that adapt quickly to unseen tasks from limited interaction, but this requires representing task evidence over long interaction histories. Classical recurrent agents compress experience into hidden states, while transformer-based agents retrieve from past trajectories at quadratic memory and computation cost. In this work, we propose Memory-Enhanced Test-Time Adaptation (METTA) for meta-reinforcement learning. It decomposes the fast adaptation phase into two temporal scales: a causal neural memory performs fine-grained within-episode adaptation with linear sequence complexity, while compact episode-level summaries support long-range cross-episode task inference without attending over the full interaction history. Moreover, equipping agents with neural memory provides sufficient expressive capacity and test-time training capability, enabling effective adaptation to unseen held-out tasks and task families at meta-test time. On unseen held-out Meta-World ML10/45 test environments, METTA achieves a success rate over $50\%$ while maintaining a low training budget and short wall-clock time. On long-context sparse-reward PointMaze tasks, it outperforms transformer-based baselines by exhibiting more structured exploration behavior. These results show that METTA provides an efficient path toward more adaptive and scalable long-context meta-RL.

Cost-Aware Abstention for LLM-Based IT Ticket Classification

Sachin Gupta

cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, Artificial, Intelligence, (ICTAI, 2026)., 9, pages,, 5, figures,, 9, tables

Wrong automatic routing of an IT support ticket can cost more than human triage. We evaluate cost-aware abstention as an offline, cost-conditional readiness assessment: select a routing policy on calibration data under explicit costs, then evaluate that frozen policy once on held-out test data. On a public 47,837-ticket, eight-class corpus, we compare four trained non-LLM classifiers with four large language models under zero- and few-shot prompting. Under the illustrative cost model (c_ok, c_triage, c_wrong) = (0, 1, 5), TF-IDF with logistic regression and a threshold of 0.60 selected on calibration reduces test cost from 0.763 to 0.472, a 38.1% relative reduction (paired ratio-bootstrap 95% CI [35.7%, 40.4%]), while lowering wrong routes from 15.3% to 3.5%. Frozen calibration-selected policies also beat both always-route and always-defer on held-out test data in 14 of 16 settings in a post-hoc cost-parameter analysis. The strongest prompted configuration reaches 0.477 accuracy on a stratified 1,000-ticket test subset, versus 0.879 for fine-tuned DistilBERT on the same tickets. Isotonic lowers expected calibration error without reliably improving discrimination: GPT-4o-mini few-shot has one confidence value before and after recalibration and correctness AUROC 0.500. We therefore report correctness AUROC and tie-aware risk-coverage area alongside ECE. At the analytically derived break-even probability 0.80, seven prompted configurations defer all tickets; Haiku few-shot routes only 19/1,000. An LLM-only agreement control and margin/entropy rejection baselines further separate calibration from discrimination. The findings are scoped to this dataset, prompt design, model endpoints, and illustrative costs; the work is an evaluation framework, not a new abstention algorithm or universal deployment certification.

Learning to Incentivise: Using Reinforcement Learning for Sustainable Urban Mobility

Germ\'an Pardo-Gonz\'alez, Shaghayegh Vosough, Katerina Papadaki, and Claudio Roncoli

cs.MA, cs.LG, cs.SY, eess.SY, Proceedings, of, the, 2025, 9th, International, Conference, on, Models, and, Technologies, for, Intelligent, Transportation, Systems, (MT-ITS),, IEEE, Intelligent, Transportation, Systems, (MT-ITS),, IEEE,, 2025

Traffic management has traditionally focused on toll-based and road pricing solutions. However, road pricing often raises concerns about accessibility and public dissatisfaction, leading to its prohibition in some places, such as Finland. This study optimises the dynamic allocation of incentives to drivers, encouraging them to reroute onto alternative (potentially longer) paths to achieve greater societal benefit, reduced total travel time (TTT) and total emissions in the transportation network, contributing to sustainable urban mobility. We employ a multi-agent reinforcement learning approach to dynamically assign incentives to drivers to reduce both TTT and emissions, with travel times estimated using traffic simulation software. We demonstrate that, with an unlimited budget and an objective of minimising travel time, the incentive scheme reduces TTT by 16%, compared to the dynamic User-Equilibrium (UE) with a budget equivalent to about 11% of the UE total time. When the goal is to minimise emissions, a 9% reduction in CO2 emissions is observed under an unlimited budget. We demonstrate a critical trade-off: minimising TTT leads to an increase in emissions, while prioritising emission reductions raises TTT. However, with the right combination of weights in the multi-objective function, both TTT and total emissions are improved beyond the baseline.

Consistent but Biased: Chair Personas Trade Noise for Bias in Multi-Agent Clinical Decisions

Yitong Bai (Columbia University), Chengyi Zhang (Swansea University), Ziyang Wang (Aston University)

cs.MA

In clinical systems built from several large language model (LLM) agents, one agent often chairs the discussion and makes the final decision. Whether the chair's persona prompt changes that decision, or the agreement between repeated runs that some systems treat as reliability, has not been tested with the case held constant. We ask whether the persona changes the decision or only its timing, how it affects agreement between runs, and which behaviors appear when the prompt describes only personality traits. A simulated department of seven agents discussed 20 published cases of degenerative cervical myelopathy, with the case reports replayed unchanged in every run. Only the prompts of the chair and its deputy changed (no persona, or a consultative, autocratic or avoidant one), written either as rules of conduct or as personality traits. With personas written as rules, the chair chose to operate now in 93% of runs when autocratic and in 2% when avoidant, against 57% without a persona. Surgery was still planned in nearly all decisions, so the personas mainly changed whether to operate now or after more tests. These effects held with a second model and reworded prompts. The autocratic and avoidant personas made the three runs of a case agree more often, but only about as often as chance predicts, and Fleiss $\kappa$ did not rise. Prompts that described only personality traits kept more than half of the autocratic and avoidant effects on operating now. They reproduced quick decisions, delays and extra tests, but not ignoring red flags, dropping backup plans or passing the decision to the family. Studies should report and vary the chair's persona as they do the model, and correct agreement between runs for chance before calling it reliability.

Decentralized Bi-Level Optimization for Cooperative Orbital Transportation of a Rigid Payload

Sidhdharth D. Sikka, Revathy Ravuri, Hei Yin Jowett Chan, Tim Echtermeyer, Shaoshuai Mou

eess.SY, cs.MA, cs.RO, cs.SY

Long-lived agents act on memory carried across sessions, and many of their actions are irreversible, so a stale memory can turn directly into harm. Existing work improves the memory itself --- retrieving, refreshing, or reconciling entries --- yet whether an entry must be re-checked before acting is not a property of the entry: the same remembered address can be used unchecked for a routine shipment but should be re-confirmed before a valuable one. We therefore study \emph{action-conditioned memory verification}: given a candidate action, decide which of its memory premises to re-verify and when to proceed or abstain, balancing execution risk, abstention, and verification cost. We formulate this as a sequential stopping problem, in which a verification is worth its cost only if it can change what the agent does next; under suitable verifier reliability and cost conditions, increasing action consequence can move the same memory through proceed, verify, and direct-abstention regimes. Building on this formulation, \textsc{AMV-Guard} is a practical verification gate that extracts the premises a candidate action rests on, calibrates beliefs about whether they are wrong, and selects checks by bounded value of information (VOI). On ToolSandbox, relative to a static policy given the same inputs, it lowers mean verification cost by 33.9\% while keeping 100\% safe-proceed recall and 0\% Gate harmful, and raises Proceed coverage from 38.83\% to 44.17\%; in a gate-core transfer to AppWorld, which carries over the stopping/VOI core but not the full structured pipeline, it lowers verification cost by 21.3\% at a small cost in safe-proceed recall and task success. Their relative priorities over the surveyed attributes, however, did not predict this weight. Reasons given for choices reflected perceived attributes more than measured features. Participants also differed stably in how much they weighted several concrete features, especially length, but with a median of 6 conversations per participant, these weights were estimated poorly. The largest gain from personal information in predicting held-out choices was 0.13 percentage points, compared with 1.11 for a stronger pooled model. Stated priorities thus track perceived attributes of AI responses but say little about the concrete features that ratings reward, a distinction that matters for preference elicitation and AI personalization. arXiv:2610.30794 (*cross-listing*) Date: Thu, 22 Oct 2026 06:19:39 GMT (2740kb) Title: VLTest: Finding Joint Image-Text Failures in Vision-Language Models Authors: Bin Duan, Jintao Lin, Guowei Yang Categories: cs.SE cs.AI ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Agents that call tools from a shared catalog can pool what they learn about which tool serves which request. Shape queries ask how a series moves. They are the main challenge of this task. Recent work makes them feasible by using symbolization as a bridge between the query and the long history. However, existing symbolizations were designed for compression, similarity indexing, or classification. What a symbolization should provide for natural language querying has not been systematically studied. We therefore examine the definitions of six classical symbolizations against this task and identify three limitations. (1) Direction is missing from the string, (2) direction is measured at too small a scale, and (3) the letters are arbitrary. Three design principles follow. We follow these principles and make a minimal redesign of SAX called TT-SAX (two-track SAX). The level letters stay. Each segment gains a trend letter. It is measured across neighboring segments rather than inside one, and graded to match the adjectives that queries use. We conduct extensive experiments on the public NLQTSBench benchmark, comparing TT-SAX with the six classical symbolizations across six LLMs. TT-SAX outperforms all six on shape queries, and ablations trace its gains back to the three limitations one by one. This paper is the first systematic study of time series symbolization with natural language querying as the design goal. It provides a foundation for future research on this task. We formulate this task as an online constrained optimization problem in which the goal is to minimize the frequency of expert auditing while guaranteeing that the fraction of AI errors that go unaudited stays below a user-defined threshold. We derive a family of algorithms with provable error-control guarantees with no assumptions, including a method that is optimally efficient: it asymptotically attains the best achievable auditing rate for both adversarial and i.i.d. data streams. Experiments on screening harmful requests to large language models, qualitative text annotation, and data labeling show that our methods achieve valid error control in practice while substantially reducing the need for human auditing compared to baselines. arXiv:2610.31663 (*cross-listing*) Date: Thu, 22 Oct 2026 17:59:29 GMT (2955kb) Title: Phase Transition from Random Features to Fully Trainable Networks Authors: Xin He, Huan Lao, Xiliang Lu, Shijun Zhang Categories: math.NA cs.LG cs.NA math.ST stat.ML stat.TH ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Autonomous multi-agent control systems (MACSs) enable distributed coordination in emerging applications such as smart power grids and autonomous vehicle or drone networks. In many of these applications, inter-agent communication relies on wireless networks. However, conventional MACS designs neglect the interaction between the communication and control subsystems. At the same time, conventional wireless designs reserve communication resources based on worst-case MACS requirements. Consequently, control performance is degraded and wireless resources are utilized inefficiently. To address these challenges, we propose a consensus-aware co-design approach for MACSs and wireless communication systems. We introduce a system model in which agents achieve consensus by exchanging information through device-to-device (D2D) links that share spectrum with cellular users under limited bandwidth resources. Based on this model, we formulate a joint optimization problem for consensus-topology selection and wireless resource allocation to minimize bandwidth consumption while guaranteeing consensus and satisfying the quality-of-service (QoS) requirements of both MACSs and cellular users. Although the resulting problem is a mixed-integer nonlinear program (NP-hard in general), a structural property of the optimal solution enables a sequential two-stage optimization algorithm with polynomial complexity. Simulations on a smart power grid case study demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves 24-27% bandwidth savings compared with the two baselines while maintaining consensus and satisfying all QoS requirements of the control agents and the communication users. arXiv:2610.30221 (*cross-listing*) Date: Wed, 21 Oct 2026 16:39:07 GMT (5916kb) Title: Agentic Architecture of Information Systems. The AAM/5C Model: A Reference Model for Distributed Autonomy Authors: Laurent Goujon (IBM France) Categories: cs.SE cs.MA Comments: 120 pages, 19 figures, 15 tables, 3 listings. Conceptual volume. English translation of the French original (version V7.83; HAL: hal-05820640; Zenodo: 10.5281/zenodo.23494375). This English edition is also on Zenodo: 10.5281/zenodo.23494664 ACM-class: D.2.11; I.2.11 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Agents can create rich representations, yet these representations are rarely used as a medium for expressing user intent. We present Agent-Bound UI, an interaction model that separates reusable interaction structures from task-specific meanings. Agents bind task-specific meanings to interactions in reusable views and publish the resulting bindings as shared state, enabling humans and agents to understand and act on shared surfaces whose meanings vary across tasks. We implement ABRA, a framework-agnostic prototype of Agent-Bound UI, in which users and agents collaborate through chat and shared interactive canvases. We demonstrate ABRA through a representative use case and evaluate four LLM-backed agents on canvas authoring, owner-side consequence resolution, and cross-agent invocation. The code and demo video are available at https://github.com/megagonlabs/abra. arXiv:2610.30864 (*cross-listing*) Date: Thu, 22 Oct 2026 07:43:24 GMT (449kb) Title: Consensus-Based Distributed Filtering for Angles-Only Tracking in Cislunar Space Authors: Giovanni Romagnoli, Fabrizio Pilone and Giordana Bucchioni Categories: eess.SY cs.MA cs.SY Comments: Accepted for presentation at IEEE NAVICON 2026 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ Robotic swarms must coordinate across diverse missions, yet existing controllers often require redesign, retuning, or retraining when objectives change. Here we show that probabilistic energy modeling provides a unifying representation for versatile swarm control, in which diverse missions emerge from compositions of shared interaction principles rather than task-specific control formulations. By exploiting the correspondence between Gibbs random field energy minimization and structured reward maximization, we encode fundamental swarm behaviors as reusable energy terms within a unified probabilistic objective. Decentralized policies trained within this representation are composed into a single controller through lightweight policy gating, without retraining. An interpretable action-attention mechanism captures non-uniform local interactions, while dimensionless normalization supports transfer across swarm scales and platforms. Demonstrated on aerial swarms, the unified policy generalizes zero-shot across six mission classes, 10-100 agents, two hardware platforms, and indoor cluttered and GPS-denied woodland environments. Large-scale simulations achieve 0.96-1.00 task success rates with 1-10 ms per-agent inference latency, while physical deployments confirm zero-shot transfer to real platforms and unstructured environments. These results establish probabilistic energy modeling as a scalable and transferable framework for decentralized swarm control. arXiv:2610.31540 (*cross-listing*) Date: Thu, 22 Oct 2026 16:48:09 GMT (2036kb) Title: Dispersive Tree Agreements for Multi-Robot Coordination Authors: Shashank A. Deshpande, Blake T. Edwards, Jonathan P. How Categories: cs.RO cs.MA math.OC Comments: 19 pages, 5 figures, 7 tables. Submitted to IEEE Transactions on Robotics ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ The rapid growth of the space economy, alongside increasing orbital congestion and debris, is driving demand for reliable on-orbit transportation of large and non-cooperative objects. Performing such transportation with a swarm of small spacecraft distributes risk and removes the single point of failure inherent to monolithic architectures, but requires solving a non-convex trajectory optimization coupling every agent's cone-constrained thrust to the payload's rotational dynamics. Existing approaches span decentralized control laws with asymptotic guarantees but limited constraint enforcement, and centralized and partially centralized planners that reintroduce a single point of failure. In this work, we present a decentralized bi-level framework driven by exact gradients: the problem is split into an inner convex thrust allocation and an outer search over payload torque trajectories. This decomposition yields fuel-efficient solutions quickly, without requiring full NLP convergence, and at short timescales its performance scales with the number of agents. Theoretical convergence guarantees are established, and the method is validated through extensive Monte Carlo simulation, including closed-loop recovery from in-flight faults. These properties make the framework well suited to spacecraft swarm applications.
